Deep Learning is a specific subfield of machine learning: a new take on learning representations from data that puts an emphasis on learning successive layers of increasingly meaningful representations. The term deep refers to there are many layers between the input and output, allowing the algorithm to use multiple processing layers, composed of multiple linear and non-linear transformations.

There are different architectures such as deep neural networks, deep belief networks and recurrent neural networks with applications in fields such as computer vision, speech recognition, natural language processing, etc.

This function is based on internal parameters of the model such as the weights and the biases. The current error is typically propagated backwards to the previous layer where it is used to modify the weights and bias in such a way that the error is minimized. This is the key of this algorithm. Okay, this is the idea. The formula is here. This cost function is based on weights, so this is the generalization of the gradient descent algorithm. This algorithm has some variants, let's check them. I'm going to start with batch gradient descent. In general, the approach is the same. It uses the entire dataset to compute the error surface and then follow the gradient to take the path of steepest descent. Batch gradient descent is sensitive to subtle points, it's a flat region, which can lead to premature conversions. It is something like this. This is a flat area, this is a representation of subtle points. When you are moving in a flat area, you are not improving the cost, you are stacked in an area. It is a representation taking in consideration only one weight, but in real cases, these points are in a high dimensional surface. This representation is useful to understand the problem, so we need to reach this minimum point, but this algorithm taking the whole dataset to calculate the gradient is sensitive to these subtle points, so we have the risk to get stacked in this flat region. Now, let's check another variant of gradient descent, this is called stochastic gradient descent. This algorithm performs a parameter update for each training sample. We are taking only one sample at a time. Due to these frequent updates, parameter updates have high variance and causes the loss function to fluctuate to different intensities. It helps to discover new and possible better local minima. The problem with ACD is that due to the frequent updates and fluctuations, it complicates the conversions to the exact minimum and will keep overshooting due to the frequent fluctuations. Although, it has been shown that as we slowly decrease the learning rate, ACD shows the same conversions pattern as standard gradient descent. So decreasing the learning rate, we can control this ACD algorithm, and performing that, we can reach in less time the minimum. In stochastic gradient descent, you have many fluctuations, but finally the algorithm converges to a minimum value. Now, let's see another variant of gradient descent, minibatch gradient descent. This algorithm is an improvement to the standard gradient descent and ACD. It takes the best of both techniques and performs an update for every batch with "end". "End" is a number of samples in each batch. We are extracting a number of samples of the total batch. It reduces the variance in the parameter updates. It's very good. So we can have a better and stable convergence. It's very useful in deep learning libraries for computing the gradient in an efficient way. The minibatch sizes are in a range from 50 to 256, but can vary as per the application and the problem being solved. You can try with different values. Minibatch gradient descent is typically the algorithm of choice when training a neural network. This is a representation that conversions is very fast and efficient. We don't have huge variance on the training process. We are going directly to the minimum. Ok, that is all for now. In the next lesson I'm going to make a review of different algorithms beyond gradient descent. There are more sophisticated models that we can use to optimize our cost function. Hello guys, welcome back to the course. In this lesson I'm going to make a review of advanced optimizers beyond gradient descent. Let's start with second order optimizers. In second order optimization, the algorithm uses the second order derivative of the cost function, which is also called Hessian, to minimize or maximize the cost function or loss function. The Hessian is a matrix of second order partial derivatives. The travel is the second order derivative is costly to compute so it is not used much. It tells us whether the first derivative is increasing or decreasing, which hints at the function's curvature. It doesn't ignore the curvature of surface, the surface of the cost function and in terms of statewise performance is better. With the second order derivative we have the information of the curvature of the cost function. Sometimes Newton's second order optimization technique outperforms first order gradient descent techniques because second order techniques will not get stuck around paths of slow conversions around saddle points. This is because we have the information of the curvature of the surface. It's very clever, this algorithm, but the problem is about the computational cost because we need to calculate the second order derivative. Let's continue with more advanced optimizers. We need to find an algorithm to reach the minimum in an efficient way. Okay, what is momentum? Momentum accelerates SGD by navigating along the relevant direction, this is important, relevant direction, and sustains the oscillations in irrelevant directions. In other words, all it does is add a fraction that is represented with this gamma parameter of the update vector of the path step to the current update vector. This is the equation. This part is from SGD, this is the same, and it's the learning rate, this is the gradient. We are adding a term that keeps the information of the path values so we can update the parameters using the path values. Usually this term is set to 0.9. You can try with different values, but this is a recommendation. This term is called the momentum term. This concept is very similar like inertia in physics. So, the momentum term increases for dimensions whose gradients point in the same directions and reduces updates for dimensions whose gradients change directions. This means it does parameter updates only for relevant samples. This reduces the necessary parameter updates which leads to faster and stable conversions and reduced oscillations. This is because the inertia representation. Okay, let's see another one. Nested off accelerated gradient. I have a situation. What actually happens is that as we reach the minima, the lowest point of the curve, the momentum is very high and it doesn't notice to slow down at that point due to the high momentum which could cause it to miss the minimum entirely and continue move up. It's something when you are in a car with a high speed, if you need to stop in a close point because your high momentum, it is possible to pass the point to stop. This problem was noticed by Yuri Nesterov. In this method, we first make a bit jump based on our previous momentum then calculate the gradient and with this gradient, we can make a correction which results in a parameter update. So, this is a way to regulate the momentum. Now, this anticipatory update prevents us to go too fast and not miss the minima and makes it more responsive to changes. It's a more clever approach. We know that we will use our momentum term, that is that, and this is the value of the momentum in the past stage to move the weights. Computing this term does give us an approximation of the next position of the parameters, which give us a rough idea where our parameters are going to be. So, this is the expression, the update of the parameters of the weights is the same using the previous weights minus this term, and this term uses the information of the previous value. But now, let's see a different approach, AdaGrad. It simply allows the learning rate to adapt based on the parameters. This is another approach, the learning rate is going to adapt based on the parameters, so it makes big updates for infrequent parameters and small updates for frequent parameters. For this reason, it is suitable for dealing with sparse data, it uses a different learning rate for every parameter, w is the weight, at a time step based on the past gradients which were computed for that parameter. AdaGrad modifies the general learning rate at each time step tip for every parameter, w, based on the past gradients that have been computed for w. The main benefit of AdaGrad is that we don't need to manually tune the learning rate, automatically it's computed the learning rate based on past gradients. The default value of the learning rate is 0.01, the main weakness of this algorithm is the learning rate is always decreasing, because this term is continuously increasing. This learning process is good in the starting, but after training many cycles, this term will tend to be 0, so the learning process is stopped due to the accumulation of the gradients in the denominator. Now let's see another algorithm, RMSprop, let's see the definition. In this algorithm, root mean square propagation, we are improving the AdaGrad algorithm, we are fixing the accumulation of the gradients, taking only the gradients from the most recent iterations. To perform that, we need to calculate this GT term, but in this case it's calculated by exponentially decaying averages of squared gradients, and not the sum of gradients. This term has an exponentially decaying, we are introducing a new parameter beta that is equal to a decay rate, Hinton suggests beta set 2.9, and this term epsilon is a tiny value. This parameter prevents the zero division, because if this term is zero, the division of this term between zero is going to be infinite, so this term helps us with this infinite situation. Also Hinton suggests the learning rate is set to 0.001. Ok, it's very good, now let's see the ADAM algorithm. ADAM is Adaptive Moment Estimation, it's another method that computes adaptive learning rates for each parameter. In addition to storing an exponential decaying average of past squared gradients, ADAM also keeps an exponential decaying average of past gradients similar to momentum. We are creating new parameters that estimates the mean and the variance of the gradients. The formulas are the following, this parameter MT is this and this parameter BT is this. I have the same parameter as before to prevent the zero division, I'm also entering new beta parameters, the recommended values for beta 1 is 0.9, for beta 2 is this value and this is the recommended value for this parameter is a very tiny value. I can use the same learning value as before, ADAM works well in practice and better to other adaptive learning method algorithms, as it converges very fast and the learning speed of the model is quite fast and efficient and also it correctifies common problems such as vanishing learning rate, slow converge or high variance in the parameter updates which leads to fluctuating loss function. The idea to present you different algorithms for optimization is to have different tools for different problems. All of these algorithms work well in different situations, so you can try with many of them. In previous lessons we have seen different architectures of neural networks and also optimizers to perform the backpropagation algorithm, but these optimizers work with different loss functions, so we can have two different loss functions, a convex function or non-convex function. Let's see these definitions, what is a non-convex function? This is a non-convex function. In this situation we have different local minimums and I have one global minimum. The task of optimizer is to reach not the local minimum but the global minimum, so the idea is not to get stacked on any local minimum, the idea is to reach the global minimum. On the contrary, we have a convex function, this is a parabolic curve, we can identify a global minimum, there is only one global minimum, it's more easy to reach this global minimum, but we can apply different cost functions depending on your problem. We have cost functions for regression problems and cost functions for classification problems. So in regression you need to predict a numerical value and in classification you need to predict a specific class for the data. But what is the utility of loss functions? I have a situation, to make the model better at classifying or predicting we must somehow change the variables for weights and biases, we know that. To do this we first need to know how well the model currently performs by comparing the predicted output of the model to the desired output. So to perform this comparison between the predictions and the real values we need a loss function. It is not only the difference, in regression we have the following, min_square_error measures the difference between the values predicted and the values actually observed. We also have the root min_square_error, that is the square root of MSE. Finally, I have the min_absolute_error, that measures the min_absolute_error between predicted and target values. This is the formula for min_square_error, I need to calculate the difference between the real value and the predicted value, the square difference for all points of data and I'm dividing by the number of samples. Because of that, I have a measure of the min, the min of the square error. Next is the root min_square_error, that is the same formula as before, but applying the square root in the previous formula. Finally, the min_absolute_error, it is only the absolute value of the differences, divided into the number of samples. In all cases, I'm dividing into the number of samples, because I'm calculating a min value. Okay, these are the main loss functions in regression. Now let's see the loss functions in classification. First I have that cross entropy is a performance measure used in classification. That cross entropy is a continuous function that is always positive and if the predicted output of the model exactly matches the desired output then the cross entropy equals zero. Only positive values starting in zero when there is a match between the prediction and the target value. Cross entropy loss increases as the predicted probability diverges from the actual label, the value is higher when the difference is high, the goal of optimization is therefore to minimize the cross entropy so it gets as close to zero as possible by changing the weights and biases of the model. This is a formula, this is a cost function so when you increase this probability you need to reduce this other term and vice versa, the idea is to minimize the whole expression. If you have more than two classes you need to apply this expression, where p is the probability to find a specific class and q is the probability to not find this class. So p plus q is equal to one. You need to apply the multiplication of these two terms in all classes. In binary classification you need to use this formula where y hat is the predicted label and y is the true label. This term cross entropy was created in the information theory field by Claude Chanel and this term represents how much information we need to know and know value. This cause in information theory is about bits, bits of information that we need to know in uncertain values of the variables in a model. Ok, let's continue. The callback labeler is very similar to cross entropy, in fact is a relative entropy because it focuses on the extra number of bits needed to encode the data. It measures the difference between probability distributions p and q, KL divergence will never be less than zero, it's only equal to zero if p and q are equal, it's very similar as before, I'm dividing the p distribution, that is the real value and q distribution is the predicted value, I'm applying the logarithm of this division multiplied by the probability of the true label distribution. Due this operation of division I'm calculating the extra number of bits. Finally, the Hinge loss is very used in support vector machines. This Hinge loss is a convex function, this is the definition, it's the maximum between zero and one minus the predicted value multiplied by the true label. The graphical representation is this, the minimum value of this function is zero because I'm calculating the maximum of these two terms, so the result will be always positive for all given numbers. This is a convex function, but KL and cross entropy are non-convex functions. [Music] Hello guys, welcome back to the course. In this lesson I'm going to make a review of different regularization techniques, but I'm going to start reviewing the concept of overfitting and underfitting. Let's start! Overfitting occurs when a model is too complex. We have too many parameters relative to the number of observations. A model that has been overfit has poor predictive performance as it overreacts to the minor fluctuations in the test data. The problem is in the test data because the model is very well fitted in the training data. When you want to perform a prediction, the predictions are very poor and this is because we have too many parameters. The opposite problem is the underfitting problem and this occurs when a model cannot capture the underlying trend of the data. This trouble means the model must have more variables to explain the data. In the following graph we can visualize these two concepts. I have two classes, the red dots and the blue dots. The purple line is an example of the underfitting problem because this model is too simple. This model can perform the separability of the classes. On the contrary, I have the green line that represents the overfitting problem because this model is too fitted to some points of data so this model can perform good predictions for unseen data. And this model represented in this black line will perform better predictions because it performs better the separability of the classes for training and testing data. Finally, if you train your model when you have the overfitting problem after some epochs, the error is increasing at some point between the testing and training data. Ok, the most common problem in machine learning is the overfitting problem so we need some options to deal with this problem. The main approach to solve the overfitting problem is the regularization technique. So, let's start. I'm going to present you three different kinds of methods for regularization. What is regularization? I can define this term as a collection of techniques that can be used to prevent overfitting. Regularization adds information to a problem in the form of penalty against a complexity to find simplest models that explains the data. What is the goal? It's to minimize the sum of the unpenalized cost function plus a penalty term, which can be understood as adding bias and preferring a simpler model to reduce the variance, getting smaller weights. Then we penalize large weights. In machine learning, our goal is not to depend on specific weights, we need smaller weights. Finally, a penalized model is about trade-off because we reduce the variance and tend to avoid overfitting. The overall result might generalize better to test data or unseen data. And this lambda parameter is a parameter that controls the strength of the penalty. Okay, this is the definition of regularization. Now let's see three different techniques of regularization with lasso, ridge, and elastic net. L1 penalty penalizes the model by the sum of the absolute values of the coefficients, while L2 penalty penalizes by the sum of squares. These are the formulas. L2 calculates the squared sum of the weights. L1, the absolute value of the sum of the weights. L1 penalized model is called lasso, while L2 is the ridge model. When you are using both in a model, the name is elastic net. So the final formulas will be this. I'm calculating the difference between the target value minus the predicted value, the squared difference, and I'm adding a term. In this case, for ridge, calculating the squared sum of the weights, and for lasso, I'm calculating the absolute value of the sum of the weights. I'm applying this term that controls the magnitude of this penalized term. These approaches are very common in machine learning. Now let's see another approach, very useful in deep learning. Data augmentation. With this approach, we are preventing the overfitting problem. The idea here is to add information to our data. But how can we add this information? There are some techniques. Example, we are talking about images. So we can flip images horizontally and vertically. You need to apply this transformation in your current data. So you will generate more instances in your current data. You can also apply a rotation. One key thing to note about this operation is that image dimensions may not be preserved after rotation. So take care about this. Scale, the image can be scaled outward or inward. Crop, we're cropping a section of the original image and resizing this section to the original image size. Translation just involves moving the image along the x or y direction or both. Gaussian noise. Overfitting usually happens when your neural network tries to learn high-frequency features, patterns that occur a lot that maybe not be useful. So Gaussian noise, which has zero mean, essentially has data points in all frequencies, effectively distorting the high-frequency features. Let's see an example. This is an image of a cat. I'm applying different transformations of this image. Each transformation now will be a data. I have a picture of 250 by 200 pixels. In this example, I'm applying a rotation, a translation, I'm cropping the image, and if I want, I can apply a Gaussian noise. So this approach is to prevent overfitting. And there are more techniques, but the latest state-of-the-art regularization technique in deep learning is dropout. I'm going to explain you this technique in the next chapter when I will teach you about convolutional neural networks. Ok, that is all for now. In the next lesson, I'm going to explain you about activation functions. Until then, review the content. For any doubt or question, you can write me on qferns. See you in the next lesson. Bye bye!

Hello everyone, welcome back to the course. In this lesson I'm going to make a review of the main activation functions in neural nets. Ok, what is the utility of activation functions? Let's see again the basic idea in neural nets. We have inputs, these are the weights for each input, after that I need to calculate this function. This function calculates the inputs by the weights, the result is called net input. This result is the input of an activation function, and this activation function performs a transformation of the input, generating an output or an activation result. In some models if this value reaches a specific threshold, this function will return an activation value. In the other cases this function will not fire any signal, so the activation is zero. So the utility of this activation function is to make a transformation of the input signal, that is the net input. With this transformation we can represent nonlinear problems. Ok, there are different activation functions, but remember that I'm writing the same formula, this is a formula to update the weights. I have my previous weight minus the learning rate multiplied by the partial derivative of the cost function. This partial derivative is with respect to the weights, but this cost function, as you know, is a function that is based on these previous activation functions in different layers. So, it's very important when I apply this derivative, take in consideration the derivative of this activation function, because if this derivative tends to zero, the whole gradient will also tend to zero, so the weights can't be updated. On the contrary, we need higher values of this gradient, usually the highest value tends to be one, so with this value the learning process is fast. Remember, the learning process is the updating of the weights. Ok, you must take in consideration these important factors to select the correct activation function for your neural net. Now let's see the main activation functions that we can apply. Let's start with a simple activation function, that is the linear function. As the name implies, it is a straight line function where activation is proportional to input. The output is a range of activations because I have the whole range of numbers, but in this case we can apply the max value or the softmax function to decide the output value in a layer. The derivative that is very important as you know of this function is a constant, is equal to one, so the gradient has no relationship with the x values and the descent is going to be constant, a constant gradient. If there is an error in prediction, the changes made by backpropagation is constant and not depending on the change in the input delta. A delta is a variation in the backpropagation algorithm. Another trouble is each layer is activated by a linear function, so no matter how many layers we have, the final output it will be a linear function and the use of many layers with this linear function it is equal to have only one layer with a linear function. It is a replication. Let's see another function to represent nonlinear problems. We have the sigmoid function that is a function very used in classification problems. This is the shape of this function. In this case it is a nonlinear function. The output value is a range between zero to one. We don't have negative values in this function. The advantage is to have values between zero to one that can represent probabilities. If you see carefully, this function tends to bring the activations to either side of the curve above two and minus two, making clear distinctions on predictions. The gradient is high on this area, but there is a potential problem with this function because for values greater than two, the y values tend to respond very less to changes in the x values. So the gradient on this area in both sides are very small. If a gradient is very small the learning process is also very low. So this problem is called vanishing gradients because these gradients tend to be zero and the learning process is stacked. This function is very useful in classification problems. Now let's see another function, hyperbolic tangent or tang. This function is very similar to the sigmoid function, but in this case for negative values, the y values are also negative. So the range is between minus one and one. This function is very used in classification problems when you need to separate very well two classes, assigning one class to the number minus one and the other class to the value one. The gradient of this function is stronger than sigmoid because the derivatives are steeper. This area of this function is steeper, so the use depends on your requirement of gradient strength. It has the same problem as the sigmoid function that is the vanishing gradient on these areas, these flattened areas. Ok, let's continue with another function. In this case I have the reloop function. This function has the number zero for negative inputs. So reloop is a nonlinear function and helps in the sparsity of the activation. For instance, if we have a big neural network with a lot of neurons, using a sigmoid or tang will cause almost all activations will be processed to describe the output of a network. So in that case the activation is dense, too many neurons are working at the same time. This is costly. The ideal situation is to have a few neurons in the network to not activate and thereby making the activation sparse and efficient. So when you have these negative values, this part of this function will return zero. So there are some neurons that are not working and other neurons are working with the positive values. Reloop is less computationally expensive than tang and sigmoid, but it has a problem. The problem is about this area, the zero value, because of the horizontal line for negative values of x the gradient can go towards zero and the weights will not get adjusted during descent. This is called dying reloop problem. Additionally, the mean output is not zero. That is the case, a positive mean introduces a bias for the next layer, which can slow down the learning. If the mean value of activation is zero, we get a faster learning, because if the mean is around this point, the derivative of this function starts with the number one, so the learning process is faster. Ok, this function is very helpful in convolutional neural networks, transforming the input data. Many functions have advantages and also disadvantages, but the use of these functions depends on your problems. This function has some variants, let's see them. The leaky reloop. This is a variant, in this case instead to have the zero value for the negative inputs, we have a little slope in the negative values. Leaky reloop is when you use this slope, 0.01. For positive values in the input data, that function is the same as reloop, so the main idea to have this function for the negative value is to avoid the value of zero in the gradient, so we can continue with the learning process. Let's see another variant, eloop. It is based on reloop. In this case, the negative part of the input data is represented with an exponential function, with a limit in -1. This function, which speed up learning in deep neural networks and leads to higher classification accuracies, like reloop, also avoids a vanishing gradient because of this part of the function. However, this function is an improvement because it has a specific function on the negative values. With this part of this function, the mean of the activation is closer to zero. Zero means speed up learning, because they bring the gradient closer to the unit natural gradient, the value of one. So, this part balances the whole function, pushing the mean of the values closer to zero. Ok, these are the main activation functions. Now let's see a summary of these functions. These are the main functions. This is the shape of the function, the equation, and the derivative for each function. In the first case it's equal to one, in the second case it's a parabolic function, which has the maximum value in one. Take note, the derivative function is based on the original function. That is the same case in the tan, and in reloop the derivative is zero or one. Zero for the negative values, and one for positive values. This is a parametric reloop function. In the case of leaky reloop, we have set this alpha parameter in 0.01. That derivative is the value of this parameter for negative values, and one for positive values. Ok, that is all for theory. In the next lesson I'm going to perform a comparison between three important activation functions. We are going to see the behavior of this function in the learning process and the loss function. I'm going to perform the code in Keras. So, review the contents. See you in the next class. Bye bye!

Hello everybody, welcome to a new chapter, this is a chapter 3. In this chapter I'm going to explain to you convolutional neural networks, or in short, confnets. What is this? Confnets are a special kind of deep neural networks, specially designed to extract features from the images in order to perform image classification. These feature extractions are also the base for object detection. In this chapter I'm going to start explaining to you the image fundamentals in computer vision, then we will review the fundamental blocks behind confnets, and finally I'm going to present you different powerful architectures. I'm going to review the theory and perform many practical applications, so let's start with the concepts of image fundamentals in computer vision. It is something like this, you know very well what is an image, but in computer vision images are represented in pixels. Let's see the definition. Every image is a set of pixels, an image is a multidimensional matrix, so imagine a grid into this image, each small square is a pixel, in this example I have 960 pixels in the white and 639 pixels in total, so this is the total number of pixels, so in a grayscale image, each pixel is a scalar value between 0 and 255, where 0 corresponds to black color and 255 is the white color. Values between 0 and 255 are varying shades of grey, where values closer to 0 are darker and values closer to 255 are lighter. Pixels in the RGB color space are no longer a scalar value like in a grayscale, because the pixels are represented by a list of three values, one value for the red color, one for green and one for blue, this is in short, red, green and blue. So we need to define the amount of red, green and blue in a single pixel. Each channel has values defined in the range 0 and 255, where 0 means no representation and 255 full representation of the color. When I refer channel, I'm referring to these primary colors, each channel is for each color. Ok, let's see this representation of the channels. That representation of the channels is something like this. For instance, I have a picture, this picture has 4 pixels in width and 4 pixels in height. There are 3 color channels, one for red, one for green and one for blue. These are the main colors, so the intensity of each primary color is represented with these numbers. When 0 represents no representation and 255 a full representation of the main color. In the minutes dataset we have seen only grayscale images, in that case we had only one matrix. But when you are dealing in color images you need to deal with 3 channels, each channel for a primary color. The notation is very similar with values between 0 and 255. Ok, let's continue with the RGB format. These are the main colors, red, blue and green. With these 3 main colors we can generate new colors. The RGB color space has an additive property, the more of each color is added the brighter the pixel becomes and closer to white. Adding red and green leads to yellow, red and green the intersection is the yellow color, adding red and blue the intersection is pink, adding red, blue and green the intersection is white, adding blue and green the intersection is light blue or turquoise. In this format we can define a RGB image as consisting of 3 independent matrices, each matrix for each channel with width and height values, one for each of the RGB components. The shape will be W, that is the width, by height by D, where D is the number of channels. For RGB color the format is D=3, because I'm dealing with 3 primary colors. Take note, in OpenCV the format is different, this library stores the pixel values in blue, green and red, a BGR format, so when you are working in OpenCV you need to make a transformation of this format from RGB to BGR. Ok, let's continue, these are good reference for RGB format, let's check the first one, this is the webpage, this is the red color, as you can see in RGB format I need to specify 3 values, this is red so the value is 255 for red, the full color in red, the resource values are 0, so this is a fundamental color, but I can play with different numbers, for example this 37 is close to 0, so it's a darker color, in the primary red color, if I use 0 this is black, 0 0 0 is black, I can play with different numbers, for example 201 in red, 155 in green and 202 in blue, this is something like a pink color or violet color. There are other formats that you can use, but in this course I'm working with RGB format. Now let's see another webpage in the reference, this is the webpage, you can access clicking on this option on the left side, there is a palette with different colors, if I select this I have this color and the values in RGB format, these are the values, if I select another one I have the new values, you can find different percentage of the selected color, this is the selected color in bold, but you can select different options, for example this or this or a lighter color, it's very helpful. Okay, let's come back to the presentation, now let's see the image coordinate system, in computer vision we need to use a coordinate system to represent the position of each pixel in the image, this is a representation, I have an image, I have values in the width, values in the height, an image is represented as a grid of pixels, the upper left corner corresponds to the reading point 0, 0, this is the reading point, as we move down and to the right both the x and y values increase, images are represented in NumPy arrays with shape, height, width and depth, depth is referring to the number of channels, the reason is the matrix notation, rows and columns, so each image is represented in a matrix notation, the number of rows in an image is this measure, the height, this is the number of rows and the number of columns represents the width of the image, in a grayscale format I only have these two dimensions, in the lower right corner this point, the total number of pixels are in width and in height, okay that is all for this lesson, in the next lesson I'm going to start explaining you the architecture of convolutional neural network for image classification, Until then, enjoy with deep learning, see you in the next class, bye bye.

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. In this class I'm going to explain you the architecture of convolutional neural networks for image classification. Let's start with the concept of image classification. What is that? In this picture I have different objects, a bike, a car and an animal. If I present this picture what is the correct label for the animal, cat or dog? It is obvious that is a dog, but it is obvious because I'm human. But for machines it is not easy, this image is only numbers with a lot of pixels. So let's see the definition. Image classification is the task of assigning a label to an image from a predefined set of classes or categories. Our task is to analyze an input image and return a label that categorizes the image. The label is always from a predefined set of possible categories. Our model also assigns multiple labels to the image with probabilities, such as dog 99%, cat 1%, the sum of all probabilities is equal to 100%. So the purpose of this chapter is to perform image classification, and to do that I'm going to explain you a deep learning model that is convolutional neural network or in short ConvNet. Image classification is one important task in computer vision. So let's start, let's see the representation. This is a generic form of convolutional neural network. I have an input image, in this case it's a car. So I need to classify this image into a specific class. These are my classes, track, stop sign, a car or a boat. So my model will perform a classification assigning a specific probability to each class. In this case, the highest probability is the car class assigning 95%. In the rest of classes, the probability is very low. So my model will assign this label to this image. This is the task to perform. Let's see the definition. Convolutional neural networks are a special kind of multilayered neural networks. We have seen multilayered neural networks in the past chapters, now this is a variant. Like almost every other neural network, they are trained with a version of the backpropagation algorithm. The difference is in the architecture. Convolutional neural networks are designed to recognize visual patterns directly from pixel images with minimal pre-processing. They can recognize patterns with extreme variability, such as handwriting digits, characters, and with robustness to distortions and simple geometric transformations. This model was created by Mr. John LeCun. This is the father of a convolutional neural network. The first model has the name LENET. Ok, the difference in a convolutional neural network is the application of many layers that act as filters. And after applying these filters, we have input data to a common multilayered neural network. This fully connected layer is a multilayered neural network, but at the beginning we need to extract some features of this image, and to perform that we need to apply different filters, such as the convolutional layer, the pooling layer, and others. So the idea is to extract meaningful information of this image, and I can enter this information to a multilayered neural network to perform the classification as usual. So we are going to reduce the dimensionality of this image, extracting meaningful features to perform the classification in a multilayered neural network. The first layer performs the extraction of the meaningful information, and the last layer performs the classification using this meaningful information. Ok, this model has some terms that you need to learn. Let's check them. What elements are in ConvNets? First, the filter. The filter, kernel, or feature detector are the same terminology. It's a small matrix used for features detection. A typical filter on the first layer in a ConvNet might have a size 5 by 5 by 3. 3 is the number of channels in colored images. So what is this filter? In this first image, this square represents a filter. So with this filter I'm extracting information of a small area. This is a convolutional operation. Next, convulsed feature activation map or feature map is the output volume formed by sliding the filter over the image and computing the dot product. Next, receptive field is a local region of the input volume that has the same size as the filter. It's the local region where the filter is applied. Depth is the number of filters, in this case is referring to the number of channels, not this filter or feature detector. Depth column is the set of neurons that are all pointing to the same receptive field. Next, stride is an entire number and has the objective of producing smaller output volumes spatially. If a stride is equal to 2, the filter will shift by the amount of 2 pixels as it convulses around the input volume. Normally we set the stride in a way that the output volume is an integer and not a fraction. Common strides are 1 or 2 and common strides 3 or more. So the idea is to pass the filter or feature detector into the image but using a stride. We are moving this small window with a stride in 1 or 2. Zero padding. This operation adds zeros around the outside of the input volume so that the convolutions end up with the same number of outputs as inputs. If we don't use padding, the information at the borders will be lost after each conv layer, which will reduce the size of the volumes as well as the performance. So with this zero padding, we are preserving the information at the borders. Ok, next. Relu layer applies the relu function as activation function using the maximum number between zero and the linear function, which turns negative values to zeros. These layers doesn't change the size of the volume and there are no hyperparameters. This activation function is very used in convolutional neural networks. I'm going to explain to you in detail in the following lessons. Next, pull layer. This is another kind of layer in ConvNets. Performs a function to reduce the spatial dimensions of the input and the computational complexity of our model, and it also controls overfitting. It operates independently on every depth slice of the input. There are different functions such as max_pulling, average_pulling or l2_norm_pulling. However, max_pulling is the most used type of pulling, which only takes the most important part of the input volume, the value of the brightest pixel. Ok, that is all for this lesson. This lesson was only a general overview of the main components in ConvNets. In the next lesson I'm going to start explaining you the convolutional operation. Until then, review the content. See you in the next lesson. Bye bye.

Let's see the representation. In this example, this is our image. I have five pixels by five pixels. This small window with two pixels by two pixels is our convolution filter. This filter is applied to our main matrix. This matrix is a representation in numbers of our image. So, the idea is to apply this filter into this image performing the dot product between the filter and the receptive field. The receptive field, as you know, is the area with the same size of the filter but this area contains the information of the image. We need to perform this dot product along the width and in the height with a specific step. For instance, if I have these values and I start in the upper left corner the first operation that I need to do is to perform the dot product between this small filter and the values in the main matrix with the same shape as the filter. So, the dot product is the multiplication of these four values by these four values. The operation performs the multiplication in the same order multiplying each pair of numbers. Two by two, this is the expression, plus three by one, plus zero by one, and finally one by five. The result is twelve. This result, twelve, is the first number in the output matrix. The next step is to move this filter in a stride. In this case, if I apply a stride equal to one I'm moving this window in one pixel. So, in the second operation I need to take this area containing the numbers one, two, five, and three. After that I need to perform again the convolution operation multiplying the values of my filter by the numbers on this new region. So, the operation will be equal to two by one, two, three by two is six, plus two is eight, zero by five is zero, one by three is three, eight by three is eleven. You have the second number. So, in the next step you need to move again this filter one stride. You need to apply the convolution operation in this new area. The result is thirteen and finally the last step is to apply the operation in this last area. The result is also thirteen. When you end all columns you need to start in the next row. The stride in the y-axis is one. So, the new area is this one, five, two, and one. The operation is two by one is two, three by five is fifteen, fifteen plus two is seventeen, zero by two zero, one by one is one, seventeen plus one is eighteen. So, you need to move again your filter one stride to the right and so on until the end of the columns. After that you need to start again but moving your filter one stride in the y-axis repeating this operation until the end of the rows. So, performing all operations you will have a new output matrix. The initial matrix is five by five, the output matrix is four by four. Take note in computer vision because we are dealing with images or video the amount of data is very huge. So, with this operation I am extracting the main features of the data. Let's see the definition. We have a two by two convolutional filter operating in the input matrix five by five with stride one in the width and in the height. The result is four by four matrix, the output matrix. We can define the input volume that is equal to the width by the height by the number of channels. The hyperparameters that we need to set up is the number of filters, the stride in this example is equal to one but you can apply different numbers of a stride and the amount of zero padding. In this case we are applying zero padding because the filter fits in the main square in all cases. Finally, we can calculate the output volume size with these formulas. In this example the new dimension in the width is equal to the previous dimension in the width that is equal to five minus f is the size of the filter in this case is two. Five minus two is three plus two by p, p is zero padding so this term is zero. Three divided by s, s is the stride in this case is one. Three plus one is four, the number four in the width, if I apply the same in the height I will have the same number four so this is the output volume size, four by four. There are some variants in the convolution operation, let's see them. Delayed convolutions, what is that? Delayed convolutions introduce another parameter to convolutional layers called the deletion rate. This defines a spacing between the values in a kernel. Kernel or filter is the same. A three by three kernel with deletion rate of two will have the same field of view as five by five while only using nine parameters. This delivers a wider field of view at the same computational cost. They are very used in real time segmentation is used when you need a wide field of view and cannot afford multiple convolutions or larger kernels. Let's see the representation. This is the convolution operation. We have seen this case but now if I apply a deletion rate equal to we are separating the filters along the pixels. In two I have a filter but not in all regions. I'm separating the filters in two pixels. If I have a deletion in three we are separating the filters in a distance of three pixels so if I have this filter three by three I can perform a deletion three multiplying the value containing on these pixels by this filter in the same convolution operation as before but only taking these numbers on these pixels. Okay, we have seen the convolution operation but now let me illustrate you how the filters look like and present you the concept of sharing weights. Okay, let me present you an example of filters. These are 96 filters. Each small square is a filter. The size is 11 by 11. This example of filters was created by Krzyzewski. In this example we are going to apply this filter in an input image with the size 55 by 55. The size of this image is related with the number of neurons. The total of this multiplication is the number of neurons needed to represent the input image. So there is a concept related with the filters. That is the parameter sharing assumption. That is if detecting a horizontal edge is important at some location in the image it should be useful at some other location as well due to the translational invariant structure of images. There is therefore no need to relearn to detect a horizontal edge in the 55 by 55 distinct locations in the convolutional layer output volume. We are going to apply the same filter in the whole image. So each filter is specialized to detect a specific kind of feature in the input image. Now let's see more in detail the sharing weights. Let's see a representation. I have an input image with 227 by 227 by 3 input volume. So I am applying a convolution filter that is equal to 11 by 11 by 3. The same depth as the input image. I am specifying these hyperparameters for example stride=4 the filter size is 11 padding 0 and k is the number of filters 96 filters. As you know we can calculate the output volume with this formula. So applying this formula the output matrix is this 55 by 55 by 3. I am reducing the input data so in this case I am applying one filter but I have 96 filters. So this is the output volume for only one convolutional operation. In this part of the graph I am representing the filters filter 1 and filter k. So for each filter I am performing the convolution operation. So the output will be different matrices with a size 55 by 55 but each filter will be used on the entire image to extract a specific feature of the image. We have 96 filters so the total output volume will be 55 by 55 by 96 filters. These numbers acts as a weights because I am performing the dot product. So these weights are applied in a local dimension in 2D. We are sharing these weights for this input image. We have different filters to extract different features of this image. The concept of parameter sharing or weights sharing is used in convolutional layers to control the number of parameters. Unlike fully connected layers, each neuron in the conv layer will only has local connectivity to a local region of the whole image. We can reduce the number of parameters by making one reasonable assumption. That is, if one feature is useful to compute at some special position then it should also be useful to compute at a different position. Each filter convolves on the input image and creates a 55 by 55 matrix, 2D matrix. The K filters together create the output of the first convolutional layer which is 55 by 55 by K. K is the number of filters. Each of the 55 by 55 neurons for each depth slice of the output from the first convolutional layer use the same weights. These are the weights 11 by 11 by 3. The intuition here is that each filter represents a feature and it makes sense to apply the same filter for the entire image using the same weights. For example, if we want to detect horizontal edges in an image, we shouldn't have to learn how to detect the horizontal edges at every single one of the 55 by 55 distinct locations. With this wearing sharing we can use the learned feature detectors anywhere. So the general idea of sharing weights is to reduce the dimensionality of the data. So at the final we can sync this process of applying filters to extract for each filter a specific kind of feature. You'll need to create the filters. TensorFlow and Keras have a way to generate filters. You only need to specify the number of filters that you want to apply. Okay, that is all for now. In the next lesson I'm going to perform an example applying different kernels in an input image. Until then, enjoy deep learning. See you in the next class. Bye bye.

Hello guys, welcome back to the course. In this lesson I'm going to explain you about the pooling operations, in particular max pooling. In the previous lesson we have seen the convolution operation. With this layer we can extract the main features of the input image, applying different convolution filters. But as you know, the output volume of data is huge, so we need a new layer to reduce the dimensionality of the data. This new layer is the pooling layer. There are different kind of pooling operations, but the most common is the max pooling operation. So let's see it. Let's see an intuitive idea. In max pooling, as the name implies, we are taking the maximum numbers in a region. My original matrix has a size 8x8 pixels. I'm applying a filter 2x2, so I'm starting in the upper left corner. In max pooling we are going to take the maximum value. For example, if I represent the maximum value in a region with a darker color, this black color in this pixel is the highest value of this small region. The second value in order is this gray value, and the last value is the white color. So what is the highest value in this area? It's this color, the color black. So the representation of these four values is the color black. This is my output matrix. The first value represents this area. The second value behind the first is also a black color. This color represents all values of this second area, because the darker color is also a black color. In the third case is the same. So I have a black color that represents this area. In the last area, all colors are white. So the highest value of these colors is the same, the white color. In this case, I'm applying a stride equal to. I'm moving my filter in two pixels each time. So if I move my filter to the right in two pixels, I'm performing the operation in this area. What is the highest value of intensity in this area? It's the white color, because all pixels have the same color. So the representation is a pixel with a white color. The same for the rest areas, white, white, and white. If I move to the right two pixels, I have this area. What is the highest value? It's this gray color in two pixels. So the representation of this area is this gray color. In this area behind this, all pixels have the white color, so the representation is the white color. Behind this, I have again the gray color as the maximum color. The representation is in gray. Finally, I have four pixels with the white color. The representation is in a pixel with white color. In the final column, the highest intensity is in black. So the representation is a black color. Behind this area, I have four pixels in white. The representation is white. Behind this, I have two pixels in black, two pixels in white. The highest value is black. So the representation is in black. And finally, all pixels are in white. The representation is white. The output matrix will be a reduced matrix of the original matrix. But taking only the maximum values that represent meaningful features of the original image. Now let's see an example with numbers. For instance, I have this example. I have an image four by four pixels. I'm applying a kernel two by two, starting in the upper left corner. So I only need to identify what is the maximum value on each region. The maximum value of this region is the number five. So I will take this number representing this area. In the second area, the maximum number is the number five. In this third area, four, two, two, and six, the maximum number is six. In the last area, one, three, seven, and seven, the maximum number is seven. So the output matrix is two by two in this case and has the maximum numbers that represents the small areas applying a filter. OK, a pull layer has two hyper parameters. The filter size in this case is two by two and stride. In this case, I'm applying a stride equal to two. If the input has this shape, the pulling layer produces a volume of size with these parameters. We can calculate the output volume after applying this operation. W2 is the width in the output. That is equal to W1, that is the original width, in this case is four, minus F is the size of the filter, in this case is two, four minus two is two, divided into the stride. The stride in this case is two. I'm moving my filter in two pixels each time. So two divided by two is equal to one. One plus one is two. So the width of the output is two. The same operation in the height. In this case, the result is also two. And D is the number of channels that is equal in all cases. We are not performing a transformation in the number of channels, only in the width and height. So, this is the idea. It just keeps the maximum value of the internal zone it covers. [MUSIC PLAYING] Hello, everyone. Welcome back to the course. In this lesson, I'm going to explain to you about the relu function. We have seen the relu function. But in this class, I'm going to explain to you the utility of this function in ConvNets. This is a function. For negative values, this function returns the number 0. And for positive values, the function returns the identity function. In ConvNets, this function is applied after the convolution operation. Remember, the convolution operation is a linear transformation. But with this function, we can perform non-linear transformations. So reloop activations are the simplest non-linear activation function you can use, because there are other functions, but this is the simplest function that we can use. But why we need this simple function? Because the high volume of data will have a high volume of computations. So we need a function to not overload the computational tasks. The reloop layer allows the ConvNet to account for situations in which the relationship between the pixel value inputs and the ConvNet output is not linear. We can represent non-linear relationships with this function. Reloop results in much faster training for large networks. This is because we have a lower computational cost. This leaves the size of the volume unchanged. When you enter the information into this layer after the convolution operation, the output of this layer is the same size as the input. However, this simple solution is not always perfect, because reloop units can be fragile during training and can die. This is a dying problem in reloop function. For example, a large gradient flowing through a reloop neuron could cause the weights to update in such a way that the neuron will never activate on any data point again. If this happens, then the gradient flowing through the unit will forever be zero from that point. For example, you may find that as much as 40% of your network can be dead. Dead, in this case, is referring to the neurons that never activate across the entire training data set if the learning rate is set too high. But with a proper setting of the learning rate, this is less frequently an issue. Remember this formula. This is the updating of the weights using gradient descent. If this gradient is too high, the multiplication of this gradient by the learning rate is a term. If this term is too high, perhaps it's higher than the previous weight. So in this operation, that is a subtraction, the result can be negative. But if we have a negative value, the reloop function will return zero. So we have two situations to control-- the size of the gradient and the learning rate. If this learning rate is too high, the multiplication with the gradient will also be high. So perhaps we are producing negative weights. Because of that, the idea is to control this learning rate to avoid these negative expressions. As you know, there are other variants of the reloop function, for example, the leaky function or illoop function. But this function, because it's simplicity, is very used in confnets. So in conclusion, we are going to use this function because it's very simple to account in nonlinear situations. We have a lower computational cost than other activation functions. And also, we have a faster training in large networks. Finally, setting the appropriate value for the learning rate, we can avoid the dying problem in this reloop function. OK, that is all for this lesson. The next lesson, I'm going to explain to you about batch normalization. Until then, review the content. See you in the next class. Bye-bye.

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class, this time I'm going to explain you about batch normalization. What is this concept? Let's see it. Batch normalization is a technique for improving the performance and stability of neural networks. The idea is to normalize the inputs of each layer in such a way that they have a mean output activation of zero and a standard deviation of one. We are normalizing the data. This is analogous to how the inputs to networks are standardized. We know that normalizing the inputs to a network helps it learn. This is the idea. We are accelerating the process of learning, because a network is a series of layers where the output of one layer becomes the input to the next. We normalize the output of one layer before applying the activation function and then feed it into the following layer. This procedure has the following benefits. You can train faster because the converge is more quickly. Another benefit is to allow higher learning rates. Gradient descent works with small learning rates for the network to converge. As networks get deeper, gradients get smaller during backpropagation and require even more iterations. So with batch normalization we are increasing the speed of the training. Another benefit is to make weights easier to initialize and also simplifies the creation of deeper neural networks provides some extra regularization. In some cases work as well dropout. Dropout is a technique for regularization. I'm going to explain you this technique in the next lesson. Makes more activation functions viable. You can use more activation functions regulating the values going into each activation function. Nonlinearities that don't work well in deep networks tend to become viable again. This technique is very useful in convolutional neural networks. Now let's see a sebdo code in Keras to implement this technique. First I'm going to need this function, batch normalization from Keras. I'm going to create a new model with sequential. Using the modularity approach I only need to use the function add to my initial model. So I'm creating a layer with 64 neurons and to apply this technique I need to use an instance of this function, batch normalization. It is usual to define the activation function as a parameter into this function dense. But you need to apply this normalization function before the activation function. So in this case I'm avoiding to specify the activation in this first layer. After that I'm applying the batch normalization and after that I can use the activation function, in this case reloop. The next step could be to use a regularizer, in this case dropout. This is the first layer. I can define another layer with the same approach. I only need to specify the input shape in the first layer. This is a hidden layer, I'm specifying the number of neurons. After that I'm using the batch normalization to have the mean in zero and the standard deviation in one. After that I can use the activation function and the regularizer. This is the last layer specifying the number of neurons that is equal to the number of classes. I'm going to perform again the batch normalization and the last activation function in this case is softmax when you have more than two classes. Finally the same instructions as usual. I need to compile the model specifying a loss and optimizer and I need to use the function fit to train our model. The procedure is very similar as before, you only need to take care about the use of this batch normalization in the sequence. Take note, I'm using an instance of this function batch normalization without any parameter, but if you want to dive deeper you can check different parameters for this function. Let's jump to Keras and see this batch normalization. This is the webpage in Keras for batch normalization. As you can see there are many arguments that you can use, but in most cases it is enough to have an empty instance of this function. But you can apply different arguments such as the axis specifying the axis that should be normalized, a momentum term, the default momentum term is 0.99, an epsilon argument is a small flowat add to variance to avoid dividing by zero. This is the default value 0.001, center true, I can apply a scale of the data if I want and there are other parameters that you can apply. The output shape is the same shape as the input, this is important. Now let's see this batch normalization but in tensorflow. This is the webpage in tensorflow for batch normalization. The approach is very similar like in Keras, the normalization process is ruled with this equation, I'm subtracting the mean of the values and I'm dividing into the standard deviation. In this case, this gamma parameter is a scale parameter. And I can also use a beta parameter that is an offset. You can apply different arguments, x is the input tensor, the min is the min of the tensor, a variance, an offset parameter, a scale, a variance epsilon parameter, that is a small flowat number to avoid dividing by zero and a name if you want, this is optional. This function will return a normalized scaled offset tensor. Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. In this class I'm going to explain you that dropout regularizer. We have seen different regularizers in the previous chapter to avoid overfitting, but dropout is the latest state-of-the-art regularizer that you can apply. Let's see the representation. As you know, in neural networks, all neurons are connected by weights between layers, so the information is flowing through the network passing for all neurons. There is a relationship between each neuron and the neurons in the next layer, so the approach in dropout is to set some neurons as an inactive. These neurons in red don't deliver any signal to the next layer, so the information flows only to the neurons that are active, in this case these neurons in white. So we are going to define a percentage of the total neurons to be inactive. With this algorithm we are preventing the overfitting problem very efficiently. This is the definition. In dropout, while training randomly, we only are keeping a neuron active with some probability p. p is a hyperparameter to be set. This forces the network to be accurate even in the absence of certain information. It prevents the network from becoming too dependent on any one of neurons. Dropout is one of the most effective regularization techniques for neural networks. Now let's see the implementation of dropout in Keras. Let's jump to the Keras webpage. This is a webpage in Keras for dropout regularizer. The implementation is very easy. The arguments that I need to set up is the rate is a flow at between 0 and 1, a fraction of the input units to drop, a noise shape is a 1D integer tensor representing the shape of the binary dropout mask that will be multiplied with the input, and I can also use a seed, a python integer to use a random seed. Ok, it's very easy. The main hyperparameter is the rate. Now, let's see the dropout implementation in tensorflow. This is the webpage in tensorflow for dropout regularizer. To implement this approach, I need to use tensorflow n.dropout. I have these arguments, x is the data, keep underlined prof is a scalar tensor with the same size as the input values, that represents the probability that each element is kept, is very similar to the rate parameter in Keras. I can also use other parameters, a noise seed and a name. This function will return a tensor of the same shape of x values. Using this probability outputs the input element scaled by 1 divided into this probability, otherwise outputs 0. The scaling is so that the expected sum isn't changed. After applying different filters, regularizers and activation functions, we need to present the information to a neural network to perform the classification. This neural network is called fully connected layer, because the input neurons of this neural network are connected with the input data that is the result of the previous layers. Let's see the definition. Fully connected layers is where the final decision is made. At this layer, the ConvNet returns the probability of a specific class. Connect every neuron in one layer to every neuron in another layer, so it's a common multilayer neural network. The last fully connected layer uses a softmax activation function for classifying the generated features of the input image into several classes based on the training data. Let's see the representation. For instance, I have an image with 227 pixels in height and in width with three channels. After applying many convolution layers, pooling layers, activation functions, batch normalization and regularizers, the output volume could be something like this, a 3D volume. So the total number of pixels in this case is a multiplication of these three dimensions. The result is 9216 pixels. We are connecting the input layer of this neural network to this input volume of data, but to perform this connection, we need to change the shape. We can deal with this 3D data, so we need to apply a transformation of this volume in a flattened array. This vector will contain 9216 values in only one column. So this new format will be the input to a fully connected layer. In this example, I have 4096 neurons that are connected to this input. After that, I have another layer with 4096 neurons. This is another hidden layer. And finally, I will have a layer to perform the classification using the softmax function. This last layer will have the number of neurons according to the number of classes. If I have 1000 classes, I'm going to need 1000 neurons. Ok, this is the idea. The connection between the previous layers and the fully connected layer. This connection is performed with a flattened array. So, let's see the implementation in Keras. This is the webpage in Keras, the flatten function. It's very easy, I need to use the flatten function. This is a parameter that a format, the default value is known. So you can use an instance, an empty instance, something like this, specifying the function. As you can see in this example, we are performing the convolution operation. After that, we need to transfer the data with this flatten function and after performing this operation, we can add to our model a new dense layer to create a fully connected layer to perform the final classification. It's very simple. So the fully connected layer is a dense function in Keras. Finally, let's see the implementation in tensorflow. This is the webpage for flatten layers. This is a description, flattens the input while maintaining the batch size. This function will return a flattened tensor with shape the batch size and k is the number of features in columns. Ok, after that, to perform the fully connected layer, you need to use this function, fully connected. This is a function, this is a common layer in a neural network. Fully connected creates a variable called weights representing a fully connected weight matrix which is multiplied by the inputs to produce a tensor of hiding units. You can apply a normalizer if you want. These are many parameters that you can use. Ok, with this lesson, our convolutional neural network is complete.
