Hello everybody, welcome to a new class. Today I'm going to present you one of the state of the art convnet model for image classification. Until now we have seen different convnets models but based on stacked structure, so these convnets have different layers but in a sequence, in a pattern, one after another. The original idea in the convnets model was to develop a network with the idea that the increase in the number of layers could improve the accuracy in these networks. Because in theory we can capture more features adding more layers. But the reality was different, the increasing in the number of layers in the stacked format didn't achieve better results. And this is because an intrinsic problem about deep neural networks, which is the vanishing gradient problem. So in rescue of that the art force presents a solution for this problem which is residual neural networks. Good, this is the structure of a residual neural network. As you can see this network contains blocks, each block with different convolution operations, one after another, but at this point nothing special. But the difference is about these connections, these aside connections. So I'm going to present you a quick overview of this model, if you want to dive deeper in the theory you can review the original paper for this model that I have included into this lesson. Ok, let's begin with the problem, the vanishing gradient problem. When you are constructing your neural network you can add more layers improving the results, but it works only at a certain point. If you trace your accuracy in respect to the number of layers your accuracy will be improved with few layers, but only in a certain point. And this is because the vanishing gradient problem. As you must know every cost function is a function of the weights in a network. You can see on the right side this representation, you also must know about back propagation, when you are trying to propagate the gradients in the network you need to calculate the derivative of the cost function in respect to the weights for all layers. So you need to take the partial derivative, which is the right side of this term, for all weights in all layers. At the beginning in the last layers the gradient is high, but when you are passing the gradient through the layers the initial layers could have a very small gradient. But it will have a function which is based on the previous layers, if some term tends to zero the whole term will tend to zero. So what is the meaning of a gradient tend to be zero? The meaning is that these weights cannot learn anymore. So if you add more layers increasing the depth of the network it's very sure that you will have this problem trying to propagate the gradients to the initial layers. But how can we fix this situation? One solution is the implementation of a residual connection, let's check it out. K.Ming Heh is the author for this solution, he proposed a residual block. But how it works? Let's see on the right side this graph. We have a layer, a weight layer, after that we can apply an activation function, it's very common in computer vision the use of redo function. After that we can have another weight layer, and these three layers are very common in every convolutional neural network. But the difference here is this operation, this plus sign, because in this proposal we are going to add the input feature map which is called the identity feature map. This value is added to the output of this weight layer. If this output is represented with a function, a function of the input, we are going to add this output to the original input. This operation is a sum operation. We are going to add two different feature maps, the input before this block and the output after this block. This connection is called the identity shortcut connection. We are creating another path and after this operation we can perform another activation function such as redo. But what is the purpose of this shortcut connection? How can this connection could solve the vanishing problem? Let's check it out. Let's see a representation. On this graph L1 represents a block. This block is composed with a convolutional 2D layer, a batch normalization layer and an activation function, a redo layer. For L2 is composed only with a convolutional 2D layer and a batch normalization layer. The layer A represents the activation, so we have the activation of a previous layer which is the input to this L1 block. With this activation we are going to create a shortcut connection. This input feature will be connected to this another block, the L2 block. So, if I want to represent the output of a layer, in this example I'm using the letter C. So, what is the output of the block L1? This output is denoted with L1+1 because it's the next step to the layer L. The calculation is a product of the weights at the point L+1 by the activation of the previous layer, in this case the activation on the layer L plus a bias in the layer L+1. A linear equation, so in deep learning to break the linearity of a linear equation we use an activation function such as relu. Applying relu to the previous term we can calculate the output of the layer L+1. Now, the output of the layer L2, or the block L2, will be calculated as the product of the weights L+2 by the activation which is the previous activation of the layer L+1 plus the bias in the current layer L2. This is the output, the same linear equation as before. Now, what is the output when we apply a residual connection in the layer L+2? The output will be the relu function of what terms? The output of this layer L2 plus what term? The initial input in the layer L. So, if we express this term, the output term, in terms of its components, we have this equation, the previous linear equation that we have calculated, plus the initial input feature map. Then, if we are performing backpropagation, the idea in every single machine learning algorithm is to control the overfitting problem trying to get small weights. So, if these weights tends to zero and the bias, which is another kind of weight, tends also to zero, the whole term will be tend to zero. The only remaining term will be the input term. So, in conclusion, the activation in the last layer, this output layer L+2, will be equal to the relu function of what? Of the initial term, the input feature map. So, what does it mean? It means when the network trying to learn in a specific layer in the depth, the learning in this layer is mainly based on a previous learning. This input feature map is generated in previous layers. So, in this way, we are linking previous knowledge of previous layers to the current layers and propagating this knowledge to the forward. So, in this way, we are assuring that the gradients won't tend to be zero. So, the way to avoid the vanishing problem is to assure the knowledge in the current layer with the knowledge of the previous layer. In this example, the activation in the layer L+2 is based in the activation in the previous input layer L. So, if the gradient is very tiny in this current layer L+2, we are assuring at least to learn about the previous activation layer. With this way, we are assuring that the whole network could learn properly, not only the last layers, but also the initial layers. But there is a situation. We are performing a sum operation. We are going to sum two different matrices. We need the same shape on both. In some cases could be the same, but in another cases they are not. So, we need a solution for fixing the shapes. We have two situations. We can perform the identity block, calculating the output when the input shape is equal to the shape of the shortcut connection. In this formula, this input term represents the output activation of the convolutional block. It represents the input to the concatenation function. Don't confuse about this term. In this case, it's easy. We can add these two matrices, but when the shapes are different, we need to perform a convolutional block. What is that? We only need to perform a convolutional 2D layer applied to the shortcut connection in order to change the shape of this shortcut connection. After this convolutional operation, we can perform the sum operation with the original input, which is the output of the convolutional block, to calculate the new output. And that's it. This is the main idea in residual networks. So, let's see the results when you apply this kind of networks, comparing the results with vanilla neural networks. These charts are taken from the original paper. You can find more details in the original paper. On the left side, we have a plain neural network with 18 layers marked on this light blue color. And on red, we have the results of a plain neural network with 34 layers. In vertical, we have the error. As you can see, the error is going down until some point. When you add more layers, the results won't be improved. When you apply residual networks, you can add more layers, but with better results. For example, if you apply a residual network with 18 layers, these are the results. But when you add more layers using these residual connections, for example, 34 layers, the results are improved. Now, the constraint of the number of layers is no longer more a limit. Okay, there are more advantages for this model. For example, we can apply a bottleneck design for the blocks. Let's check it out. On the left side, we have the base block, the same as before. We have the shortcut connection, convolutional layers, and a real layer. But on the right side, we have a special bottleneck design. For example, an input feature map containing 256 filters can be transferred in a lower dimension using lesser number of filters. In this example, 64. And to do that, we use a filter with a size one by one, setting up a specific number of filters, in this case, a lower quantity. This layer acts as a bottleneck, constraining the size of the parameters in a lower dimension. When you are working with one by one filter, you are preserving the shape of the input in 2D. But because the input feature map is a 3D tensor, the third dimension, which is composed with the number of filters, will change. In this case, since 256 to 64, we are reducing the number of filters. This is because we are going to perform an element-wise multiplication using the convolution operation. So, we are wrapping up the volume. The input volume, we are creating a bottleneck at the beginning. After that, we can apply a real activation. After that, another convolution operation, for example, with a filter 3x3, the same number of channels, 64. But finally, we are returning the original input shape. The final operation is the same as before, the sum operation. But what is the advantage of this method? It is the reduce of the number of parameters. Because if you are working with the full size of the number of parameters, the number of computations will grow in a huge amount, complicating the training process. So, doing this design, we can reduce the number of parameters and in consequence the number of computations accelerating the training process, but without reducing significantly the accuracy in the model. There are different implementations of these concepts, let's check it out. As you can see, there are different blocks on the left side and here in this column is the output size for each block. And we have different models, 18 layers, set it for layers until 152 layers. So, with this residual connections we can construct more deep neural networks. In the first designs, as you can see, we only have convolutional layers, but after this model with 50 layers, we are introducing the bottleneck design. These numbers represent the number of blocks. Finally, we have a softmax operation because we are performing image classification. And this last row is about the number of computations. Finally, let's see another improved model based on the original ResNet model. We have ResNets, how it works. In this graph we have the representation on the left side, we have the original ResNet model with a bottleneck design, but here on the right side we have this new structure. In this approach we have different branches, in total 32 branches. Each branch creates a path, but after ending each path we are going to concatenate the result of all of them. And after this concatenation we are going to add the initial input feature map, the same as the original shortcut connection, so the number of paths or branches is represented in a new hyperparameter with the name cardinality. And the experimentation with this new design is that when you increase the number of cardinality, so we are increasing the width of the network, not the depth of the network, you can achieve better results than increasing the depth of the network. So with this basic idea we can construct new models. Let's check it out. In this example there are three different equivalent models. Each layer is represented with the number of input channels, the convolutional filter and the number of output channels. So you can play with different topologies. Now let's check the results for applying different topologies comparing to the original ResNet model. Here on the left side appears the results for models with 50 layers in the original ResNet model and also in the new ResNext model. As you can see the results are better in ResNext models for training and validation. We can achieve lesser errors. The same on the right side, the evaluation contains models with 101 number of layers for the base model, for the ResNext model, in both cases for training and validation the new model achieves better results. This evaluation was performed using the ImageNet 1K dataset. Finally I'm going to present you another improvement of the ResNet model. This is DenseNet. In this approach every block of layers contains different layers but each layer is connected to the previous ones. So we are creating multi-identity mappings. We are linking the information between layers. So the first layer fits the information to the forward layers, the second layer fits information to the rest forward layers and so on. So the final layer receives the information from all previous layers. After that we apply a convolution operation, a pooling operation and we initiate another block with information of the previous dense block. You need to take in consideration that when you design a network it's very important to calculate the number of call parameters that are involved because we have a limit in the number of computations that we can perform. So if you create more connections you are adding more operations. With this basic configuration we can also construct different models with different number of layers. Let's check it out. For example there are topologies about 121 layers and also about 264 layers. These architectures were constructed to be applied for ImageNet dataset. For more information about this architecture you can review the original paper which is this. I have attached it in the resources of this lesson. There are combinations of these ideas constructing new architectures. Ok, that's all in the theory. In the next lesson I'm going to perform a practical example implementing a ResNet model with 50 layers. Until then, review the theory, see you soon. Bye bye.

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. In this class I'm going to start explaining you the concept of object detection. What is this? The idea here is to identify objects into an image, specifying the specific location and according to a list of classes. For instance, on this image we have identified several objects with a bounding box surrounding each object and according to a list of classes. In this case, we have classes about persons, cars, traffic lights, stop signs and that's it. But we don't have classes about trees or buildings or publicity panels and so forth. So, in order to clear the concepts, let's see our representation. I have different scenarios. In classification, we assign a class to the whole image. We also have different scenario, which is classification plus localization. We identify a single object into an image, specifying the localization of this object with a bounding box surrounding the object. This is for single objects. But we also have another scenario, for instance object detection. The difference here is to detect multiple objects. Here I have found it three classes, cat, dog and duck and every object with a specific bounding box. Finally, there is another task, which is instance segmentation, that consists to identify every pixel that belongs to a specific class. Here we don't use bounding boxes, rather we apply mask. So let's continue with object detection. There are several methods to perform this task, but the main state-of-the-art algorithms use two different approaches, which are one stage method and two stage method. In one stage method, we pass the whole image once and the model outputs the classes and the bounding boxes. The idea here is to focus in the inference speed. However, two stage methods are focused in the accuracy. In two stage methods, we need to pass the whole image twice, one time for detecting the possible locations of the objects. And with this information, we need to pass again the whole image to detect the classes and the bounding boxes. With this method, we achieve a better accuracy, but we penalize the inference speed. So I can make a generalization in a schema. We have images, we need to fit these images to a target region selection. The idea here is to create possible regions where to find objects. These regions are also known as regions of interest. Then we pass this information to a neural network, which is a feature extractor. This network extracts the relevant features from the candidate regions in order to perform object classification, identifying a specific class. And we also pass the information to a bounding box regressor to determine the specific size and location of the bounding boxes. In every object detection algorithm, we need to perform these two tasks, classification and regression. That's it for this generalization. So I'm going to start explaining you the beginnings in object detection algorithms. Let's see a naive approach using ConfNet for object detection. If we have a picture, the idea is to identify this object, this card. So a naive approach consists in two divides this picture in different parts, and for each part we can run a ConfNet for classification. For instance, I have this. If I split this image in four parts, I can pass every part to a ConfNet. So only in this part I can find an object, but the problem here is about the sizes or the scale of the images and also the ratios. For instance, I have this. I also have a card here. I have divided the whole image in nine parts, but as you can see, this object is also a card, but this size is much larger than this. This card occupies six areas instead of only one. Then it would be very difficult to allocate a single banding box. And also the ratio is different. Here the ratio is about 1 by 1, but here the ratio is about 1.5 by 3. If we apply more regions, for example, 1000 regions, it would be very complicated to assign a banding box because for every specific region, the ConfNet will return a class and a banding box. So in order to solve this problem, there are better methods which are known as region-based CNN, or in short, R-CNN. I'm going to explain you these R-CNN methods in another lecture, but let's finish this lesson explaining you another approach, which is selective search. This is another method for object detection, which uses the concept of pixel clustering. Here I have a representation. We have three stages starting in the stage one. The idea here is to determine regions according to the pixel information. So we start merging pixels based in the notion of similarity. There are four similarity measures which are based on color, texture, size, and fill. It's an iterative algorithm. In this first step, we have several proposal regions. Because of that, here we have several banding boxes. In the next step, we have merged different small areas into larger ones proposing less banding boxes. In the step three, we again have merged the areas creating greater ones. Then we have less banding boxes than before. And that's it! This is all about the pixel information. If you are interested to dive deeper in this algorithm, you can review the original paper. Ok, that's it for this lesson. See you in the next class. Bye bye!

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. In order to understand better the object detection algorithms and also to compare them in their results, we need to learn which metrics are applied in object detection. For instance, I have this information taken from the faster RCNN paper, so the idea is to compare which model is better. I have here two models, fast RCNN and faster RCNN. These results are based on COCO dataset, the backbone model is VGG16, these are the number of proposals, in fast RCNN is selected search 2000 proposals per image and in fast RCNN the proposals are made from RPN network, in this case only 300 proposals. Here we can see the comparison, invalidation and in the test dataset and these are the metrics. This MIP is the mean average precision metric, which is the most important metric in object detection. Here higher values are better performance, so in all metrics this faster RCNN model performs better than fast RCNN model. Ok, this is the idea, the comparison between models. Now, before diving into the concept of mean average precision, we need to review an important index, which is the Jaccard index. This Jaccard index in object detection is known as intersection over union or in short IOU. Let's see an illustration. This index is defined in object detection as the overlapping area between two boxes divided by the union of the areas of these two boxes. So here one box is represented as a grand true box and the another box is the predicted box. In object detection our goal is to predict bounding boxes very close to the grand true boxes. Here in this first scenario the locations and the sizes of the predicted box and the grand true box are enclosed, so this IOU metric is very poor with a value lesser than 0.5. Here we have a better situation, the predicted and the grand true box sound close and with a similar size, then the IOU metric is better than before with a good value and finally we have an excellent situation where the bounding boxes are very close in the localization and also in their sizes. We have an IOU value very close to 1, if bounding boxes match exactly this IOU metric is equal to 1. Keeping in mind this metric I'm going to present you the mean average precision. We have a situation to compare different models in their predictions, so how can we answer which model is better? As you know every model in object detection returns a class probability and also coordinates about the bounding boxes and here we can apply the curacy metric because it doesn't work properly for imbalanced datasets. So the first thing is to define a dataset, for instance very popular datasets are VOC 2007 or 2010 and the COCO dataset. These datasets are our base for comparison. Then this mean average precision metric was created to compare uniformly the models. This metric is based in two important metrics which are precision and recall. These are the formulas in any classification model, we have true positive, true negative, false positive and false negative values. So precision is equal to a ratio between the true object detections divided by the total number of predictions. We apply this metric for a specific class while recall ratio is equal to the total number of true object detections divided by the total number of objects. There is a trade-off between precision and recall, so in order to construct the mean average precision metric we need to define a threshold, the threshold applied to the IOU metric. If our IOU is higher than 0.5 it's considered true positive, on the contrary is false positive. For comparison we apply a threshold of 0.5 in the Pascal VOC dataset and different thresholds from 5% to 95% in the COCO dataset. So depending on this threshold we will find these values. These metrics are very important because there is a trade-off between precision and recall. So using these both metrics we can achieve more stable results. So let's see how to calculate the average precision in the VOC 2007 dataset. First I'm going to start with the average precision and after that I'm going to illustrate you how to determine the mean average precision. The dataset for reference is VOC 2007. For this dataset there is a procedure identifying the IOU metric setting the threshold for IOU in values higher than 0.5. So here we need to construct a graph plotting the precision and recall values. Recall on x-axis, precision on y-axis. To determine the average precision we need to take 11 values equally spaced from the recall x-axis. For every value in recall we find a specific value in precision, then we average all values for these 11 samples starting in 0 and ending in 1 and this is the average precision metric. Finally the mean average precision in this case is the mean for all of these average precision values but across all classes because we need to perform this calculation for each class. In this example the class is cut and the IOU threshold is 0.5. That's it. Finally let's see how to calculate the mean average precision for COCO dataset. The procedure for calculation is different. In this case we don't set only one value for a threshold then we use a range of IOU threshold values and calculate the mean average precision for each IOU taking the average at the final. This is the formula. Here these numbers represent the IOU threshold starting in 0.5 and ending in 0.95. Taking a step size in 0.05 we take the average of these mean average precision values. This is the standard notation AP for average precision a robot square brackets specifying the range of IOU values starting in 0.5 ending in 0.95 with a step size of 0.05. Another kind of notation for different IOUs is here. In this case with this IOU in 0.5 we can write average precision 50 and if this IOU is 0.75 we can write average precision 75. This metric is standard in the evaluation of object detection models. Remember this metric is for a specific dataset. The calculation is different. Now let's see another important metric which is mean average recall. This metric is based in the recall metric. It's another metric to compare the object detection algorithms. The average recall is calculated as the recalled average over different IOU metrics in a range between 0.5 to 1 but not taking discrete values like in average precision rather calculating the area under the recalled IOU curve which is expressed in the following equation 2 times by the integral between 0.5 to 1. The function is a recall function evaluated on each IOU value. In the COCO challenge this metric is evaluated per class very similar like in average precision. Finally the mean average recall will be the average of the different average recall across all classes where k is the number of classes. Very good. Finally let's see some variants for the mean average precision and mean average recall in the context of the COCO challenge. In this COCO challenge which is a dataset containing 80 classes we have the following variants. As you know in this COCO challenge the mean average precision is evaluated with different treasures. Not only one treasure like in Pascal VOC. So another variant in this challenge is to consider different object scales, different sizes in the objects. So here we consider mean average precision for small objects which have areas less than 32 by 32 pixels. We also consider mean average precision for medium objects with areas greater than 32 by 32 but less than 96 by 96 and also the mean average precision for large objects with areas greater than 96 by 96 pixels. For every variant we take the average over 10 IOU treasures starting in 0.5 and ending in 0.95. So in this way we can compare the ability of different models to detect objects with different sizes. We also have a variation for the mean average recall taking consideration the number of detections per image. So we have images containing one detection. We take the corresponding mean average recall metric. We also have images containing 10 detections as a maximum. We also have images containing 100 detections. This is also another way to compare the performance of different models about the number of detections. Finally we also have a variant about the mean average recall but taking consideration the scale of the objects. So we have the mean average recall for small objects with areas less than 32 by 32, medium objects with areas greater than 32 by 32 and less than 96 by 96 and finally large objects with areas greater than 96 by 96. The same like in mean average precision. As you can see every dataset challenge has its own metrics. Okay, that's it for this lesson. See you in the next class. Bye bye.

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. In this class I'm going to start explaining you the RCNN algorithms. The first one is region-based convolutional neural network. Let's see a representation. The idea here is we have an image, so the first step is to determine the regions of interest, the possible locations of objects. To perform that we use the selective search algorithm proposing 2000 regions of interest. This huge amount of proposals is to identify objects of different sizes and ratios, but the computational cost is very high. After creating the regions of interest you will get regions of different sizes and different ratios, but we can't deal with different sizes and ratios. Because of that we need to warp these sizes in a specific size, because we need to pass this information to a coordinate and this coordinate needs a specific size in the input. So we perform feature structure on each region of interest, then we run 2000 times coordinates into a single image. Finally, in the last layers we perform classification using support vector machines for identifying a specific class and a bounding box regressor to determine the size and location of a bounding box. These values in the regressor are specified as offset values, because it's more easy to deal with the difference between the prediction and the ground-through box. This algorithm has some disadvantages. Let's see it. First, it's a high computational cost. This algorithm can be implemented in real time, because it takes around 47 seconds for each test image. The problem here is the number of proposals. We have 2000 per image. And also we don't have a way to improve the knowledge of our model, because the selection of the region of interest is in charge to the selected search algorithm. But this algorithm is a fixed algorithm, because no learning is happening here, so the accuracy in our model is only based in the confidence. Then if there is a bad candidate, the model can fix it and learn based on the errors. Okay, this is the first approach in RCNN. Now let's check a better algorithm, which is FastRCNN. It's very similar like the previous one, but with some key important changes. Let's check the architecture. In this first step, we have a different approach. Instead of passing the input image to a selected search algorithm, we pass the image to a confnet. So, after convolving the image, we have a feature map. And now, on these feature maps, we apply a proposal method, which is the selected search algorithm, to determine the region of interest. It's very important to take note about the size of these feature maps, because after applying this initial confnet, the original image is scaled down in a ratio. Now in this stage, we have different regions of interest with different sizes and ratios. So we need to normalize the sizes of these regions. To perform that, we apply the region of interest pooling layer. Let's see what is this about. First, it's very important to be clear about the region of interest projection. Let's see this representation. This is the original image in height and width. Into this image, we have detected a dock surrounding the object with a banding box. This is the location of the center of this banding box, and these are the sizes of this banding box. Very well. This is the input. So, after passing this image into a confnet, we obtain a feature map. The size of this feature map is based in a ratio. For instance, FastRCNN uses VGG16 as a confnet. This confnet has this specific ratio, 1 by 16. This ratio means that the original sizes are scaled down in a factor 1 divided by 16. Then, we can project the original information into the feature map, determining the new location in this feature map for the center of this banding box and also the new sizes. Then we have the feature maps containing the projection of the region of interest, but as you know, this region of interest has different sizes and ratios, so we need to normalize the sizes using the ROI pooling layer. So, let's see in detail this layer. Let's check an example. Here I have the input on this square red box. I have the region proposal. The idea is to divide this original region of proposal in roughly equal regions. The number of new regions is a parameter to be determined. For instance, if we divide the original region in 4 regions, this is a roughly approach. Now for each new region we apply max pooling operation, taking the highest number into each region. Then the output will be a feature map with a standard die size containing the main features. Now with this fixed size, we can pass this information to a fully connected layer. Let's come back to the model architecture. Okay, we are here at this step in the ROI pooling layer. We pass the information to fully connected layers, flatten this information previous to the final classification and regression. We apply a softmax layer to determine the specific class and we run a banding box regressor to determine the size and the location of the banding boxes. The remaining disadvantage of this method is the use of this selected search algorithm because it produces so many proposals. Okay, that's it for this fast RCNN model. Instead of running the selected search algorithm to produce the written proposals, it uses a special network which is called the written proposal network in order to produce the region of interest. This region proposal network performs a classification but not for the class label. Instead it predicts if there is an object or not. So the classification is for background or foreground. It's a binary classification. If the objectness score predicts the presence of an object, then a bounded box register predicts the size and the location of the bounded boxes. So the main change is here. Let's see in detail this region proposal network. One important change in this region proposal network is the anchor boxes, which are predefined bounded boxes in order to fit into different objects with different scales and ratios. In specific, this region proposal network uses 9 anchor boxes. These are the scales and aspect ratios. We have a ratio 2 by 1, 1 by 1, and 1 by 2 for different types of objects, in vertical or in horizontal. We apply these anchor boxes to the feature maps, moving the anchor as a sliding window, with a stride in 16. For instance, if we have a feature map with 38 by 56 pixels, into this feature map we are going to apply 9 anchors. And considering a stride of 16, the total number of proposals will be 1,192. Okay, let's continue with more details. So we apply the anchor boxes to the feature map, then the representation is a sliding window, something like this. We pass this sliding window with a stride of 16 over the feature map, and for each step we perform classification for detecting the presence of an object. So we are going to perform 9 times for every step, because we have 9 anchor boxes. Here it's a binary classification that returns 1 or 0. Because of that, there are 2k scores at the final classification layer, where k is the number of anchor boxes. And for variation it returns 4k coordinates, where k is also the number of anchor boxes. And the number 4 represents we need 4 values to determine the size and the location of a bounding box. The goal of RPN is to determine the presence of an object or not, selecting only regions which are highly probably to have objects. Take note this classification is not for the class labels, it's only to determine the presence of an object, no matter what class is this object, because the goal in this step is to produce proposals. Ok, let's come back to the model architecture. With this RPN network we have the proposals. Now we can fit these proposals to this ROI pooling layer, because we have the same situation like in Fast RCLN, we need to standardize the different sizes and ratios of these proposals. So, what is this ROI pooling layer? Let's start checking the ROI projection. After applying a covenant to an image we have this. This is the original image with this specific size, we have the center of this image and we have detected an object which is a dot with a bounding box colored in red. This bounding box has these measures. So, the effect of applying a covenant into an image is to reduce the original size into a smaller one, producing a feature map. This reduction in size is calculated by a ratio, in this case 1 divided by 16. This is because we apply in Fast RCLN a BGG16 covenant. So, the original sizes and locations are scaled in a factor 1 divided by 16. Now, in this feature map we project the original information about the bounding box. For instance, the center now is located here and the size of this bounding box is also rescaled in this new bounding box with this new size. After applying this ROI projection we can apply this ROI pooling layer. Let's see this layer in an example. I have this, which is an input image. The RPN network has generated this region proposal, so we need to standardize the size of this region proposal, but as we have different objects with different sizes and ratios, we are going to have different sizes and ratios in the region proposal. So, we need to pass this information in a standardized size because the next layers need the same size in the feature maps as an input. For instance, if we have this region proposal marked on red, we split this region in sub-regions. This number of new regions is a hyperparameter to be determined, but the idea is to divide in roughly the same areas. So, for this example I have divided the initial region proposal in 4 sub-regions and for each new region we perform the max pooling operation, so we take the maximum value for each region. And finally we produce a standard size in the output containing the main relevant features from the original region proposal. This standard size in faster R-CNN is 7 by 7. And that's it, let's come back to the model architecture. After applying this ROI pooling layer we are ready to perform the final classification for detecting a specific class and also a regression for predicting the specific location and the size of the banding box. Finally, let's see some final details into this faster R-CNN model. A more detailed architecture is here. We have an input image, the backbone is BGG network which is a feature extractor. This feature extractor produces a feature map, for instance with a size 60 by 40 and this is the output in the number of channels, 512. Into this feature map we apply the anchor boxes, 9 anchor boxes. After that we convolve the output, the output of this 3 by 3 convolutional filter is passed to two different convolutional filters, one for classification and the other one for regression. These are 1 by 1 convolutional filters because we are preserving the information in 2D. This filter produces 2 by k number of predictions for classification. Because k is the number of anchor boxes the number 2 is referred to the number of classes, it's a binary classification. We only determine the presence of an object and in regression it produces 4 by k number of predictions because 4 is the number of parameters needed to define a bounding box multiplied by k which is the number of anchor boxes. So this structure is the RPN network, it's passed to a non-max suppression layer. What is this? Because we have 9 anchor boxes we are going to have a large number of proposals, then we need a method to reduce these proposals. This is non-max suppression layer. Let's check it out. If we have an image with many proposals, for instance in this picture we have a dog with many bounding boxes to be applied so we need to discard many of them in order to have only one bounding box, for example here with this bounding box on green. With this method we take the bounding boxes with the highest confidence score and also discarding bounding boxes with an IOU value less than a threshold. Let's come back to the model architecture. Ok after reducing the number of proposals with this non-max suppression layer we have our final proposals. This information is passed to this ROI pooling layer. Now clearly we can see these two stages of the detection model. This image is passed twice to this backbone VGG network, first for the region proposal network and second for this FAS-RCNN network because FAS-RCNN uses the same network as FAS-RCNN, also this backbone is the same VGG16. After applying this backbone to the image we also have the same feature map, like in the region proposal network. But now we incorporate this feature map to the region proposals using this ROI pooling layer. This layer produces this output volume where N is the number of anchor boxes and 7 by 7 is the standard size that produces this ROI pooling layer. 512 is the same number of channels. And now because we have a normalized shape in the feature map we can pass this information to a fully connected layer, another fully connected layer and finally we can perform the classification according to a specific class producing N by Z predictions because N is the number of anchors and Z is the number of classes. Also at the final we have a fully connected layer that performs a banding box regression, producing N by Z by 4 values because N is the number of anchor boxes, Z is the number of classes and for every banding box we need 4 parameters. The outputs are predictions which are compared with the grand truth values and with a loss function we perform again the cycle, adjusting the weights for classification and for regression. If you want to dive deeper into this model I recommend you to review the original paper. That's it for this lesson, see you in the next class, bye bye!

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. This class is dedicated for one state-of-the-art algorithm in object detection, the name SSD or Single Shot Detector. This algorithm is one stage of the detector, which means we only need to pass once the image to perform the localization and classification of the objects present in the image. This is a main difference with two-stage object detection algorithms like FASTER or CNN. In these algorithms we need to pass twice the image, one time for localization and another time for classification. So, the main idea to simplify this process in only one stage is to achieve better speed in the inference and also we can achieve very good performance in the mean average precision. So, let's dive into this model checking the architecture. This is the main architecture, we have two kinds of inputs, one architecture is for images with 300 pixels and the other architecture is for 500 pixels. The first step is to use a base network, which is a feature extractor. This base network is also called a backbone network, for SSD is VGG16. With some little changes, this backbone network is pre-trained on classification tasks, but to use this network in SSD we need to remove the last classification layers and add another layers to perform the classification and localization task. So, after passing the image to the backbone, we are going to have a feature map, but not only one feature map. We are going to create new extra feature layers with different resolutions because the idea is to capture different object sizes in the image, large objects and small objects. So, with these extra feature layers, we perform the localization and also the classification of the objects. To create these new extra feature layers, we perform convolution operations to this initial feature map. These convolution operations are one by one and three by three changing the number of channels. So, for each of these extra feature layers, we are going to predict detections. The number of detections is 8,072 boxes per class and this is a huge number. Because of that, we need to void many banding boxes using the non-maximum suppression technique. The name multibox is because we are going to have several boxes into these feature maps and perform the banding box regression for each banding box, but how can we make predictions using these feature maps? Let's see the concept of convolutional predictors. We need to focus on these extra feature layers because for each of these feature layers marked on red, we are going to make predictions. And the predictions are for the confidence, which is the probability to find an object with a specific class and also the localization of this object with a banding box. So, we are going to work with these feature maps, then let's see two of them. Here I have a representation of this feature map five by five. Then we can create a grid with five pixels on each dimension. And for each grid cell, we are going to make predictions. Taking consideration, we are using the information of this feature map, not the original image. So, this grid is for this feature map. Now let's see this feature map three by three. Now in this feature map, we have fewer cells than before. We only have nine cells. Then the size of each grid cell is greater than before. Each cell can map larger areas in the original image and also is responsible to find large objects. Now let's see how to calculate this number of detections. We start at this layer, which is the final output of the feature structure VGG16. Here we have a feature map, 38 by 38. And on this feature map, we are going to apply for each grid cell four default banding boxes. And this is the total number of predictions we are going to make. After that, we pass to this conf7 feature map, which has 19 by 19 pixels. And at this point, we are going to apply six anchor boxes. And this is the result. Then we pass to the next feature map, which is 10 by 10. We apply six anchor boxes. We calculate the total number. The next feature map, five by five by six anchor boxes. This is a total number. Next three by three by four boxes. This is a total number. And finally, the last feature map is a one by one feature map with four bonding boxes. Summing all of them, we have this number. Now let's dive into a roll of the feature maps, the small ones and the larger ones. Every feature map tracks different features from the original image. I have an example. Here we have two classes, the dog marked on red and a cat marked on blue. The cat is a small object and the dog is larger than the cat. For instance, in this picture, I have represented a feature map eight by eight pixels. This feature map tries to find smaller objects than this because here we have more grid cells, 64 grid cells. And in this feature map, we only have 16 grid cells. So this four by four feature map will try to find larger objects than this. This is because each grid cell is related to a specific area in the input image. Then greater grid cells covers large areas and small grid cells cover small areas in the input image. And as you can see, for each grid cell, we apply different anchor boxes with different ratios. So for each grid cell, we are going to predict the localization with the bounding box, determining the offset values about the center of the bounding box and also the differences in width and height. In addition, for each grid cell, we are going to calculate a confidence value, which is a probability that an object belongs to a specific class. If we have n classes, we calculate n plus one values because we add the probability that the object is a background. We incorporate the background as a class probability. And this is because we don't have a specific sigmoid classification to identify if there is an object or not. We directly calculate the probabilities of different classes. Then we add another class, which is the background class. So in conclusion, small feature maps tracks larger objects and larger feature maps tracks small objects. Okay, now let's see how it works, the cell activation. If we have two classes, one class a bicycle with a bounding box on blue and another class is a dog with a bounding box in red. So how can we determine which cell is in charge to detect the presence of an object? To perform this task, we identify the center of the bounding box. So the center for this blue bounding box is here marked on this blue dot and the center of this red bounding box is here marked on this red dot. So the next step is to identify in what cell this center is located. In this case is on this cell. And for this red center, this is the cell. So these two cells are in charge for detecting specific objects. Okay, these are the cell activations, but this is a feature map seven by seven with a specific number of channels, for instance, 512. In these channels, we have different spatial information about the image. Then we need to combine the whole volume of this feature map with convolutional filters in order to produce these activations. But how can we represent the activation? We need vectors, vectors that represent the information of the bounding box. Let's see it. We have something like this, the dog class, the bounding box, the center in red, and this is the cell activation. For each activation, we create a vector that contains the information of this bounding box. The first four fields are the coordinates of the bounding box, but using the offset values for the center of the bounding box, the offset values of the width and height. After that, we include the information of the class probabilities. For instance, if we have three classes to detect, we need to include three fields in the vector. Finally, the probability to find an object, which is the background probability, is another field to be included. Summarizing this vector, we start with the number five because we always need four values to determine the bounding box coordinates, and we also need one value for the background, and C is the number of classes. Each bounding box is represented in this array format, and taking consideration, the information is related to the center of the bounding box. On the other hand, many objects could share the same activated grid cell, so for each grid cell, we are going to need B bounding boxes. Then, if we apply B bounding boxes for each grid cell, we are going to have B by 5 plus C number of filters, and it's important to note that the spatial dimension of this feature map won't change because this information about the bounding boxes will be in the channel dimension. We affect only this dimension. Okay, we generate multiple bounding boxes. However, different objects could have different aspect ratios. For instance, this dog is about one by one, and this bicycle is about one by two. So, if eventually different objects could share the same grid cell, what kind of ratio we apply for this grid cell? A bounding box in horizontal or in vertical? So, in order to solve this question, this algorithm uses multi-box priors. The concept is very similar like anchor boxes in faster R-CNN. Let's check this concept. Multi-box priors are predefined bounding boxes with specific ratios to be applied. This algorithm specifies these aspect ratios, five different aspect ratios. Then, we are going to have B bounding boxes for each grid cell. However, it's a potential problem to generate so many bounding boxes for prediction, because we are going to have many of them with negative values. So, the initial solution is to use the IOU metric. Discarding bounding boxes with an IOU value less than 0.5. Remember, the IOU metric is calculated as the area of overlap divided by the area of the union. These areas are referred to the ground truthbox and the predicted bounding box. If we train this model with these priors, we can detect objects with different sizes. However, this metric is not enough to balance the data. We still have many bounding boxes with negative values. So, let's see how to tackle the imbalance training problem. We have an issue in the training process, the imbalance of the data. Most parts of the data are negative examples. Then, the solution to use non-maximum suppression. This technique consists to void different bounding boxes, which are classifying the same object, because we have different ratios, but at the final, we only need one prediction for each object. We keep the bounding boxes with the highest confidence score and also with an IOU metric higher than 0.5. Then, we select only the best bounding box for each object. Another technique is called hard negative mining, which consists to balance the number of negative predictions using only the false positives at each iteration, keeping a 3 by 1 ratio between negative to positive. So, we constantly include this false positive to the training data in order to force this model to learn from these missed predictions. Finally, there is a new approach, changing the loss function. It changes the arena's categorical cross-entropy loss, adding a scaling factor, focusing on hard examples during training. This loss is the focal loss. So, let's see what is the arena loss and the new focal loss. The total loss consists in two terms. One loss for the confidence in order to classify an object to a specific class and another loss about the localization of the object calculating the offset of the bounding boxes. The confidence loss is the categorical cross-entropy loss, which is also known as softmax loss. This is a mathematical term. This loss is calculated across all classes. The another loss is the localization loss, which is a loss calculated between the predicted box and the grand true box, and is defined as the smooth L1 loss. This is the mathematical term. The calculations of the offset are here for the center from x-coordinates and y-coordinates and the offsets for the width and height. We calculate the logarithm of this ratio, the division between these two terms, which are exponential terms. Then, the final loss is the sum of these two losses, but applying an alpha term, which is a hyperparameter, the mathematical expression is here, and this term n is the number of matched default boxes. If n is equal to zero, this term is also equal to zero. Finally, there is an improvement of this confidence loss, which is the focal loss. Let's see the representation. This is the cross-entropy loss for different classes, and this is the focal loss. We add this term with this gamma exponential parameter. If this gamma parameter is equal to zero, we have the original cross-entropy loss. With this parameter, we are reducing the losses for the well-classified examples, focusing on the negative predictions. Finally, the model also includes data augmentation techniques. In order to end this lesson, let's see some final considerations. This model is faster than faster R-CNN, and also has better mean-average precision in the highest resolution, with 512 pixels. And also, this model performs better than the initial YOLO model. YOLO is another state-of-the-art object detection model, which is also one stage of the detector, but this advantage is only for the first YOLO version. On the contrary, there are some issues in SSD, because similar categories are confused and also produce worse performance to detect smaller objects. This is because of the travel in the ratio between the spatial resolution and the low resolution in the final feature maps. They are too tiny to represent important features from the original image. One solution for this issue is to increase the resolution of the input image. This is a good algorithm, with a balance between mean-average precision and speed. You can run this algorithm in real time. I have attached the original paper for more information. That's it, see you in the next class. Bye bye!

Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. This class is dedicated for one state-of-the-art object detection algorithm, which is YOLO. That means You Only Look Once. The reason for this name is because YOLO is one stage object detector algorithm. It only needs to pass the image once to predict the localization of the bounding boxes and the classification of the objects. The main advantage of this algorithm is the speed at the inference, and the accuracy is not greater than SSD or faster RCNN, but is quite good. For this lesson I'm reviewing the first version of YOLO, but actually the latest version is 5. I'm going to start with the theoretical background since the initial version. The main idea here is to use a convolutional neural network, but removing the last classification layers and instead placing regression layers. The architecture was created to use in Pascal VOC dataset, which is a dataset that contains 20 classes. Let's begin checking the architecture. The architecture is inspired in Google LeNet. YOLO uses several convolutional layers to reduce dimensionality of the input image in a factor 1 divided by 32. There are several convolutional blocks using 1x1 and 3x3 convolutional layers. Remember, when you use 1x1 convolutional filter you are preserving the information of the spatial data, but changing the size of the number of channels, so it produces this feature map 7x7 by 1024 channels. This volume is flattened in a fully connected layer, and then we reshape this flattened format in a 3D volume with the sizes 7x7 that contains the spatial dimension reducing in a factor by 32 and in the channel dimension 30 channels. This channel dimension contains the information of the classes and the position of the boundary boxes. So, in these last two layers we apply linear activation functions, and in the previous layers we use leaky relu. In order to generate this output tensor, YOLO creates a grid into an image. So, let's see what is this grid. We create the grid into the input image. So, each cell has the same size in width and height, and for each grid cell we are going to predict one object, and also we are going to apply bounding boxes. For instance, in Pascal VOC the number of bounding boxes per cell is equal to 2. One bounding box is applied in horizontal shapes, something like this SUV, and the other bounding box is applied in vertical shapes like this person. Then the output tensor for each grid cell is equal to the number of cells in height and width, the same number, multiplied by the number of boxes for each grid cell, and each bounding box is multiplied by 5, because we are going to need 5 values to represent each bounding box. Finally, we add the number of classes to be detected. If the size of the grid is 7, the number of boxes per cell is 2, and the number of classes in Pascal VOC is equal to 20, the output volume following this formula is 7 by 7 by 30, which is the output value for the network. One important consideration is because every grid cell predicts only one object, YOLO has some limitations to detect small objects or objects that are too close each other. Another consideration is not all grid cells produce object detections, so let's see how to perform the localization of the objects. For instance, I have an image, I have a grid 7 by 7, and this is the object that I want to detect, a person. This object has a bounding box, then the idea is to determine the center of this bounding box, which is marked on this black dot. So, we need to ask in what grid cell this center point is located, here in the cell number 5. Then, this grid cell number 5 is responsible to detect this object. The rest of the cells have anti-values, so we create arrays containing the predictions, something like this. This Y array has the following items. The first one is the confidence factor, the probability to find an object. In the cases of the cells 1, 2, 3 and 4 we don't have an object, so the confidence score is 0. But in the case of the cell number 5 we have detected one object, then the confidence score is 1. The next attributes are the coordinates of the center of the bounding box and the next two are the height and width of the bounding box. Finally, we have the classes, the probability to find a specific class. So, in this middle array we have 0, which is the confidence score to find an object, and the rest of the values are filled with empty values. Then, we don't keep this array for calculating the loss. It doesn't matter this array, but the array that represents the cell number 5 is here, with a confidence score equal to 1, different coordinates for the center of the bounding box, different sizes in height and width, and the information of the classes. Here is the class number 2, which is detected, and the other classes are 0, because we didn't find the other classes. Every cell detects only one class, so the notation is one hot encoding format. The coordinates of the center of the bounding box are scaled in a range between 0 to 1 and the same for the height and width of the bounding box. These values are divided by the height and width of the total image. Very good. Now let's dive into the coordinate system, how to calculate these coordinates. The coordinate system is created in relation to each grid cell, not based on the entire image. Here, for this image, the zero point is here in the upper left corner. Then, we have an object, a dock, which is detected with this bounding box. The center of this bounding box is here, on this green dot, and these are the coordinates for this center, but related to this original zero point in the picture. Then, because this center point is located into this grid cell, we need to rescale these coordinates based on the coordinates of this grid cell, not based on the coordinates of the zero point of the image. So we need to identify the upper left corner for this grid cell, which is marked on this yellow dot with these coordinates. And also, we have absolute values for height and width for this bounding box, so rescaling this center and this dimension means to transfer these values in a scale between 0 to 1. This is a calculation for the new center. We have the coordinates of this center and we subtract the information of the upper left corner of this grid cell, and for width and height, we use the measure of the total image to normalize these values. In this case, 448 for height and width. And that's it. So, in conclusion, every bounding box will be represented in a tensor with these scale values and adding a confidence factor. If we have more than one box, for instance, two boxes in a grid cell, we can represent these bounding boxes in a tensor. Let's see it. For this example, I have three classes, car, pedestrian and dock. Then we need to encode the information of the bounding boxes in a tensor. For each grid cell, we apply b bounding boxes and for the Pascal VOC, which is the initial data set used in YOLO version 1, we use two bounding boxes, one in vertical and the other in horizontal, and also we apply a grid with a size of 7. Then, if this data set has 20 classes, the output will be a tensor 7x7x3. And the representation is here. We have a tensor containing the information of two bounding boxes. The first value is the confidence probability. The next four values are the coordinates of the center of the bounding box and the dimensions for height and width. As we have seen, these values are scaled in a range between 0 to 1. And finally, we include the information of the classes. We assign a field for each class. In this example, we have three classes, then we need three fields in the vector. If one box detects a car, the confidence value for detecting an object is equal to 1 and we assign the number 1 to the specific class, in this case the car class. In the second example, we also have detected an object, then the confidence score is equal to 1, but here this object is a pedestrian, then the class number 2 is assigned to 1. And the rest of the classes are 0. This process to convert the information of the bounding boxes in tensors is also known as encodings. Let's see our representation. The idea is to codify the information of the bounding boxes. I have an input image with this size in height, width and three channels. We pass this image into a convolutional neural network which acts as a feature extractor, reducing the dimensionality of the input image in a factor of 32. Then the output is a feature map. This feature map is 19 by 19. Then if we apply a grid with this size 19, the encoding for each grid cell is 19 by 19 by curly brackets 5 by 5. One 5 is because we need 5 values for each bounding box containing the information of the center and the dimensions and also the confidence score. And the other 5 is because we apply 5 bounding boxes for this example, each bounding box with a specific ratio. Finally, we add the number of classes. In CoCo dataset we have 80 classes. In Pascal VOC we have 20 classes. Then this is the whole idea of encodings. We input an image and we output tensors. Now let's dive into this confidence score. The confidence score represents the presence of an object. But how can we calculate it? We use the IOU metric. This metric is calculated as the area of overlap divided by the area of union. We apply this metric to the ground truth boxes and the predicted bounding box. Then if an IOU is higher than 0.5, we assign a confidence score equal to 1 because we have a high probability to find an object. But if the IOU metric is less than 0.5, which means we have a low probability to find an object, then we assign the confidence score equal to 0. So it's a binary score. Normally we can calculate the confidence score equal to the probability to find an object multiplied by the IOU metric. If this probability is equal to 1, the confidence score is equal to the IOU metric. And if this probability is equal to 0, the confidence score is equal to 0. This score is only for detecting the presence of an object. But here we are not specifying what kind of class this object belongs to. Then we also need to calculate class probabilities. Let's check it out. If there is an object, we need to assign this object to a specific class, calculating a probability. We create a class confidence score, which is the box confidence score multiplied by a conditional probability. Then we already have seen that the confidence score is the probability to find an object multiplied by the IOU metric. And the conditional class probability is the probability to find a class given an object. If we multiply these two terms, the class confidence score is equal to the probability to find a class multiplied by the IOU metric. If we don't have an object, we don't calculate the class confidence score. We calculate this class confidence score for each class, but not for each bounding box. Then for each cell, we calculate C class probabilities. And in total, we are going to have the number of cells by C class probabilities in the prediction. Here it's very important to note that for training, we need to pass the information of the grand truth boxes containing the information of the classes and the correct bounding boxes. Then we train the detection layers for learning the specific location of the bounding boxes and the classes. On the other hand, after applying the IOU metric, we still could have redundant bounding boxes, but we need only one bounding box for each object, no more. So the solution is to use the non-maximum suppression layer. If we have several bounding boxes trying to detect the same object, something like this, we need to discard many of them and select only the best bounding box. So the idea here is to filter the information of the bounding boxes sorting the confidence score. We filter with a threshold of 0.6. Lots of boxes are discard and for these selected bounding boxes, we calculate the IOU metric with values higher than 0.5. So with these two filters, we select the best bounding box. And that's it. Finally, we need to calculate the loss. Let's check it out. The total loss function for this model contains three terms. The localization loss measures the difference between the grand truth boxes and the predicted boxes. So the formulation is here. These first two lines are the components of this localization loss. Here we apply the sum of the square errors, the summing for all bounding boxes and for all grid cells, and only is applied if there is an object. This symbol 1 represents the presence of an object. And for these dimensions in width and height, we apply the square root of the values. This is because small deviations in large boxes matter less than in small boxes. And finally, we apply this term at the beginning of each sum, which is a regularizer in the loss. If we need to put more emphasis in the loss about the localization loss, we increase this term. The default value is 5. Very good. The next component of the total loss is the confidence loss. This is a loss based on the IOU metric between the grand truth boxes and the predicted boxes. This loss also has two terms which are here, one for detecting the presence of an object and this other term to detect the null presence of an object. This term is because in every image we have many grid cells do not contain any object, so this pushes the confidence score towards to zero, creating a disbalance in the gradient, and in consequence the model is unstable. So in order to solve this issue, we add this term that is applied to the null presence of an object, balancing the loss. So with this term we reduce this loss, that the default value is 0.5. Finally, the classification loss. If an object is detected, we need to calculate the probability of a specific class. So for this loss, we apply the sum of square errors for all classes evaluated on all grid cells, but only if there is an object on the grid cell. Finally, the total loss is the sum of these three losses. Finally, let's see some pros and cons of this model. The main advantage of this model is the speed. We can detect several objects in a picture and this high speed is evaluated in the inference time. This model can achieve 45 frames per second. The main disadvantage of this model is the accuracy, especially for groups of small objects. And this is UE, YOLO only takes one object per grid cell. This is the initial version of YOLO, there are some improvements in the YOLO version too, that I will review it in another lesson. That's all for now, see you in the next class, bye bye!
