Hello everyone, welcome to a new class. This class is for YOLO version 2, which represents an upgrade from the initial version, YOLO version 1. The idea with this new version is to improve the accuracy results as well as the speed. So, let's start checking the main improvements. For this version we apply patch normalization instead dropout layers. This layer acts as a regularizer and increases the main average precision in 2%. The model also increases the input resolution for the classifier. The initial YOLO trains the classifier with this resolution 224 by 224, but in YOLO version 2 the resolution is doubled 448 pixels in height and width, trained for 10 epochs on ImageNet dataset. This high resolution in classification increases the main average precision in 4%. On the other hand, YOLO version 2 takes a different approach about the predictions of the Greek cells. Let's check it out. YOLO version 2 uses the approach of anchor boxes, which are predefined bounding boxes with specific ratios and measures. Let's start illustrating an issue in the approach of YOLO version 1. Here I have two objects, an SUV in horizontal and a person in vertical. As you can remember, we need to identify the center of each bounding box, the center of this person is marked on this yellow dot and the center of this SUV is marked on this blue dot. Then this Greek cell is responsible to detect these two objects. So, there is an issue in YOLO version 1, because each Greek cell in version 1 only can predict one class, then we can't predict more than one object. So, in order to solve this problem, YOLO version 2 uses anchor boxes per Greek cell and also takes a different approach in the predictions. We are going to predict based on these anchor boxes, not based on a Greek cell. Then with these anchor boxes, we can manage objects with different sizes and ratios for each Greek cell. In order to optimize the predictions, there is a new spatial dimension, let's see it. Here we are going to change the size of the grid. The idea is to locate the center point of the output feature map in a specific Greek cell, because the initial resolution 448 in height and width produces even Greek cells. We will produce an output feature map with a size 14 by 14, then it will be very complicated to assign the center point to a specific Greek cell. Let's see the representation. If the number of Greek cells is even, we have this situation, the center point, which is mainly responsible to identify large objects, will be here, but which is a responsible cell for this center? We have four candidates. It would be very complicated to assign a specific Greek cell. However, if we reduce this input resolution to this resolution 416 pixels, we are going to produce a 13 by 13 feature map. The Greek cell in size is an odd number and it's very easy to identify the cell which is responsible to the center point. These final sizes in the Greek cells occur because we have a downsample factor of 32, reducing the dimensionality of the input data to the output feature map. In addition, the new architecture removes one pooling layer to increase the default 7 by 7 feature map, increasing the output to 13 by 13 feature map. Now, per each Greek cell we are going to apply anchor boxes. Let's check it out. We are going to have k number of anchor boxes per Greek cell, something like this. Some anchor boxes are in vertical, others in horizontal. Here I have a square anchor box with a specific size. This is another square anchor box with a different size. So we need different sizes and ratios because in real life the shape of the objects are very close to one of these anchor boxes. For instance, pedestrians can fit better in vertical boxes and cars may fit better in horizontal anchor boxes. Then this version of YOLO uses five different anchor boxes and now the predictions are made for each anchor box and not for each Greek cell. So we are going to predict k number of anchor boxes. For this prediction we need to remove the fully connected layers which are present in the version 1 and work with the information of the anchor boxes. The main idea here is to change the approach from the cell level to the anchor box level. Then we are going to generate the confidence score which is the probability to find an object, the class predictions which is the probability that an object belongs to a specific class for each anchor box. In order to calculate the objectness score which is the probability to find an object this version of YOLO uses the same approach as the version 1 calculating the IOU metric. This metric is applied for the objectness score and also to calculate the class prediction which is a conditional probability. We are going to calculate a specific class only if we detect an object. So each banding box prediction will contain four parameters to identify the center of the banding box and the offset value in height and width related to the anchor box. There is also a confidence score and finally the information of the classes. YOLO version 2 output has a shape 13 by 13 because we apply a reducing factor in 32 multiplied by curly brackets, k is the number of anchor boxes by curly brackets, 1 is the objectness score plus 4 which is the number of parameters required to define a banding box and here is a change, plus 20 in this case the number of classes for Pascal VOC. So take note in this case the classes and the objectness score are calculated for every banding box. This is the main difference from the initial version of YOLO. In the initial version we stack the information of the banding boxes in only one tensor because the information of the classes is the same despite the banding boxes. For this example the final output is 13 by 13 by 125. These anchor boxes produce a small decrease in the accuracy but it increases the recall metric. Now let's see how to determine this k number. How many banding boxes we are going to apply per grid cell. YOLO version 2 uses a clustering algorithm to identify the best number of banding boxes. So we are going to apply the k-minx algorithm to identify the number of groups or clusters. Here we have the plot for COCO dataset and here we have the plot for Pascal VOC 2007. This is a representation of different banding boxes grouped by similarities. These shapes on black correspond to the Pascal VOC and these light blue shapes correspond to COCO dataset. And in order to calculate the best distances in the clusters the algorithm applies not the Euclidean distance rather a distance based on the IOU metric. This distance is calculated between the box and the centroid and is equal to 1 minus the IOU metric between the box and the centroid. After running four different clusters the best results were found when k is equal to 5. This is a reason why this algorithm uses five anchor boxes as default per grid cell. On the other hand we have a new coordinate system. This approach is called direct location prediction and is represented with this image. The initial travel with YOLO version 1 is there is no constraints on location predictions. Then the initial prediction of a banding box could be very far away from the grand true box. Then the idea to direct location prediction is to constrain the location of the predicted boxes. We use the offset values for height and width with respect to the grand true box and these values are constrained with a sigma function in order to produce values between 0 to 1. And we also constrain the confidence score with this sigma function because this is a probability with values in a range 0 to 1. And the calculation for the box confidence score is the same like in version 1. It's the probability to find an object multiplied for the IOU metric between the predicted banding box and the grand true box. In this new coordinate system the point of reference is not the center of the banding box, rather the top left corner of the anchor. Now let's see the concept of fine grained features. In YOLO version 1 we have the information of the output feature map to produce detections, but it suffers from detecting small objects because the final resolution is very poor and only can detect large objects. So in order to solve this issue we incorporate feature maps with higher resolution. So Dalgo-Riden uses a feature map with a size 26 by 26 with higher resolution than the final feature maps. The idea is to add this information with higher resolution with information of the last layer with low resolution. So in order to perform this concatenation we need to change the shape of this feature map. So we convert this 26 by 26 by 512 feature map into a feature map with a size 13 by 13 with 2048 channels. This is because this higher resolution has a double size in height and width. Then we have four times in the number of channels to preserve the volume of the data. The result is 13 by 13 by 2048. So we add the information of the last layer which is 13 by 13 the same spatial dimension and the number of channels here is 1024. Then if we have the same spatial dimension we can extract the information of the channels summing them in this third dimension producing in total 3072 channels. This kind of connection is also known as a residual connection. You can check it in the ResNet architecture. In YOLO version 2 this approach is called pass-through. So in conclusion the idea is to capture with this higher resolution small objects and with this low resolution large objects. This technique improves the mean average precision by 1%. YOLO version 2 has two main parts. One part is the feature extractor or backbone and the other part is the layers for object detection. The backbone is new here which is called darknet. This new feature extractor or backbone produces better results than VGG16 and the original backbone in version 1. The architecture is here. This is darknet 19 because it uses 19 convolutional layers with 6 max pooling layers. The convolutional filters are 3 by 3 for feature extraction and 1 by 1 for changing the channel size. This architecture is used for training in classification. After that we remove the final layer and train the network for object detection. So let's see the components of the training. Here we have two training steps. We need to perform training for classification using darknet 19 trained on ImageNet which has 1000 classes. The initial resolution is 224 by 200 by 24 and then doubles the resolution for 10 epochs. For the training time this algorithm uses data augmentation using random crops, rotation and so on. After the training on classification we need to train the detection layers. The final layer of darknet 19 is removed and replaced with 3 by 3 convolutional layer with 1024 filters. And after that 1 by 1 convolutional filter changing the number of the channel dimension in order to produce this feature map 13 by 13 by 125. As we have seen this number represents the information of the banding boxes. Finally YOLO version 2 uses a multiscale training approach changing the input dimension every 10 batches using image sizes by a factor of 32. For instance 320 by 352 and the largest resolution 608. With this technique we are forcing the network to detect objects at different scales. Finally let's see the accuracy and speed results in a comparison chart. YOLO version 2 has different competitors such as SSD and YOLO version 1. We have in horizontal the speed in frames per second and in vertical the mean average precision. For YOLO version 2 the base 1 outperforms in mean average precision to SSD 300 and the initial version of YOLO. Even more the results are better than faster R-CNN, SSD 512 and faster R-CNN plus ResNet. And in terms of speed there are several architectures with better speeds than the other competitors achieving good results in the mean average precision. This evaluation is for Pascal VOC 2007 dataset. If you need more information about this YOLO version 2 algorithm you can check in detail the original paper. That's That's all for now. See you in the next class. Bye bye.

See section `Flowing the graph using flow` in https://github.com/thtrieu/darkflow.

Now, to detect objects in video, Run the command in terminal as:

```
uv sync
       
cd darkflow
../.venv/bin/python flow --model cfg/yolo.cfg --load bin/yolo.weights \
  --demo ../cars.mp4 --saveVideo --saveVideoPath ../videos_out/cars-yolov2.mp4 \
  --threshold 0.4 --gpu 0.8

```

Now, to detect objects using webcam, Run the command in 54.Deep-Learning-for-Computer-Vision-with-TensorFlow-2 folder and in terminal as:

```
.venv/bin/python yolov2_od_webcam.py
```

Run the command in terminal as:

```
cd 54.Deep-Learning-for-Computer-Vision-with-TensorFlow-2

.venv/bin/python yolov2_custom_object.py prepare
.venv/bin/python yolov2_custom_object.py train --epochs 300 --gpu 0.8
.venv/bin/python yolov2_custom_object.py video --load -1 --threshold 0.3
.venv/bin/python yolov2_custom_object.py cam  --load -1 --threshold 0.3

```

Hello everybody, welcome to a new class. In this class I'm going to present you the latest state-of-the-art algorithm for object detection, which is YOLO version 4. This algorithm uses many of the latest discoveries in the computer vision field. As you know, any object detection algorithm has two important goals, the accuracy in the predictions and the speed. The speed is represented in frames per second. How many frames per second can we take for object detection? And the accuracy is mainly denoted with the mean average precision metric. The situation here is most of the object detection algorithms meet one of these two goals, but not both, so YOLO version 4 represents a great improvement in both goals. What is new in this version 4? We have a model that can be trained in a simple GPU and also incorporate many recent techniques in computer vision, which are called backup freebies and backup specials. The main purpose is to improve the results in the precision and also the speed of the model. This chart is on the paper if you want to dive deeper in the theory behind YOLO version 4, you can check the paper that I have attached in the resources for this lesson. Previously we have seen YOLO version 3, which represents an improvement of YOLO version 2. Now, this is the latest model, YOLO version 4. It has been released on April 2020 and as you can see in this chart, on vertical we have the average precision and in horizontal the frames per second, which is the speed. In both metrics we have very good results. This algorithm represents an improvement of the previous version, YOLO version 3, because the average precision and the frames per second are improved in factors 10% and 12% respectively. Ok, now I'm going to make a quick overview about the structure of the object detector, which is present in this model YOLO version 4. After that I'm going to review the main new techniques, this backup freebies and backup specials. So, let's start with the object detector structure. When I'm referring an object detector, I'm referring to the network, the network which is in charge for extracting features from the images to perform the final object detection. The structure is here, mainly there are two kinds, one stage detector and two stage detector. In one stage detector we extract the features passing the image only once. On the contrary, in two stage detector we need to use two networks for performing the detection, which is a case of faster RCNN. In this case we have the region proposal network for extracting the features, we propose different regions and for each region we apply the whole network for object detection, but YOLO has one stage detector. In one stage detector we have these four main blocks, the input for taking the information from the image, the backbone which is in charge for feature extraction, this is a case of YOLO version 3 with the network darknet 53, ResNet, VGG16, the family of ResNet models, so this backbone is a convolutional neural network specially designed for extracting features from the image. After this module we have the neck module, this block adds more layers with the proposal to detect more fine grained features. Remember one of the problems in YOLO is about to detect different sizes in the objects, in special tiny objects. The idea here is with these extra layers and with the special architecture we can deal with different scales in the features, which is a case of feature pyramid network or FPM. So in this module we can use different approaches, for the backbone, for the neck. Finally we have the final block, which is the head. In this block we are performing the location of the bounding boxes and the final classification. As you know in YOLO, including this last version, version 4, we use vectors containing the classification for the classes, the location of the bounding boxes and the size of the bounding boxes. So in conclusion, the modules backbone, neck and head are the same for many one stage detectors. The difference is about the specific technique or network we are going to use on every module. Now I am going to continue presenting you a quick overview of the main techniques behind YOLO version 4. I am going to start with back of freebies, which is a collection of different techniques. The idea with these techniques is to improve the accuracy. There are new techniques about the augmentation, random cropping, the block. For instance, mosaic is a new technique created by the authors of this version of YOLO. This is a representation of this technique. We create new training images consisting in four different images taken from the original training dataset and making a collage of these images. In this way, we are forcing the model to learn in different contexts at each time and helping us in the overfitting problem. In the same way we have catmix but taking only two images from the original dataset. These techniques are applied in the training process. Another important technique is drop block, which is similar to dropout. Previously we have seen dropout but now drop block represents an improvement of this vanilla version. For example, we have this picture of the dog. The image in B represents the approach in the regular dropout algorithm taking randomly different pixels from the images and removing them. But the approach in drop block is not to use a certain region of the pixels. So in this way we are forcing the model to learn specific features without some part of the information. So in this way we can achieve more accuracy and also to prevent the overfitting problem. Another important technique to achieve a better accuracy is about a new loss. This new loss is complete IOU. As we have seen in previous lessons, we have a metric. The main metric for object detection is IOU. But now we are going to apply IOU not only as a metric but as a loss, incorporating this metric in the loss. We have different situations. The green box is the target box, the red box is the predicted box. If this red box is far away from this green box and both don't have an intersection, we have an IOU score equal to zero. So in many cases we are going to add different zeros in the loss and this situation is a problem because we are not improving the learning, adding zeros in the loss. So, in order to solve this problem, it has been created different metrics as a losses. For instance, we have the IOU, but takes in consideration how far away is the predicted box to the target box. This loss helps when there is no intersection between these bounding boxes. But in these three situations this loss is the same because the predicted box is contained into the target box and also in order to solve these kind of situations it has been created another metric as a loss which is called distance IOU. This metric takes in consideration the distance between the center points from the target and predicted boxes. So, in this way we can measure how precise is our prediction in relation to our target box. So, as you can see, the metric is not the same in all cases. The best case is here because the centers are aligned, outputting the lowest value in the loss. But it is not enough because we are not measuring the aspect ratio and the overlapping area. Because of that, it has been created the complete IOU loss, which is also a metric, but it is used in the loss function. This loss incorporates different factors such as overlapping area, the distance between the center points and aspect ratio. So, it's an improvement to the vanilla IOU loss. In addition, using this kind of loss we can avoid the use of regularization factors in the loss. Ok, that's all for now, I'm going to make a break. In the next lesson I'm going to continue with the theory behind back-of-face shells. Review the theory and the paper. See you in the next class, bye bye!

Hello everybody, welcome to a new class. I'm going to continue with main techniques under the hood yellow version 4. In this lesson I'm going to explain you the techniques under the name bag of specials. These techniques have the purpose to increase the speed of the model and also to improve the accuracy. Some of them are used in the neck and in the backbone. But unlike bag of freebies, these techniques change the architecture of the network because we are going to add many layers and many special connections between them. The initial point to understand these techniques is the building block of feature pyramid networks. So let's see how it works. This architecture works in the neck, linking the backbone to the head. In the left side we have a standard convolutional network composed in different stages. Each layer here represents a stage and each stage has different convolutional layers and pooling layers. In this case we have different stages reducing the size of convolutional feature maps. In a structured button app the factor of reducing on each stage is the factor of two, applying pooling layers. So in the top we have strong semantic features but with low resolution because the size is tiny. And at the bottom we have the input which is the image. This shape is very similar to a pyramid. This is the reason for the name of this architecture. So this architecture offers us, for each stage, different scales. It is used in YOLO v3 but only this structure, the left side. But the complete building block is composed with another structure in parallel, linked by lateral connections. Very similar like in ResNet. Each lateral connection merges the feature maps from this button app structure and the feature maps from this structure which is a structure with a shape top-down but way, the inverse of this original structure, and producing different pyramid levels. But before merging these feature maps we need to scale these feature maps in a factor of two. Here is the representation because on left side we have used pooling layers dividing the shape in a factor of two. So we need to match the shapes between feature maps before applying the merging operation, that is in this case the sum operation. And also in the lateral connection we have a convolutional filter with a size 1x1 extracting the feature map in 2D. If you want to dive deeper in the effect of convolutional filter with a size 1x1 I recommend you to review the lesson about ResNet and also Inception. So the final prediction is applied in the head and we are going to have different predictions for different stages which represent different scales so we can detect different object sizes. The concept behind feature pyramid networks is very similar like ResNet but including the factor of the scales. If you want to dive deeper in the theory behind this building block you need to review this paper, feature pyramid networks for object detection. And that's it for this building block. Now I'm going to continue exploring these new techniques. I'm going to start with cross stage partial connection or CSP. What is this approach? Let's see this image. We have on A a regular approach, for example a DenseNet model. In DenseNet we have different layers with different connections based on the idea of residual networks. So we have a base layer, different blocks and a transition. This approach cross stage partial connection splits the base layer in two parts. One part is preserved and the other part continues with the same procedure passing this part to the dense block to the transition block but finally the output of this splitted part is added, is concatenated to the part one in a transition operation. The idea behind this is to reduce the computation in operations because a dense block contains many layers and many lateral connections. So if we split the information of the base layer we are performing less operations because we are going to apply this dense block not to the whole base layer but only in a part. Because of that this technique has a goal to increase the speed in the model rather than the accuracy. So if we have a feature extractor like darknet 53 which is present in YOLO version 3, now with this model we convert this regular network in CSP darknet 53 and this feature extractor could be present in the backbone of YOLO version 4. Now let's see another technique, this is pad aggregation network or PAN. In this illustration we have in the option A the regular pad aggregation network, the concept is the same like in ResNet, we perform addition linking the layers with connections but in YOLO version 4 the app force uses a different operation not an addition but concatenation. Remember this operation in linear algebra when you make a concatenation we are grouping in this case two feature maps. With this operation of aggregation we are adding more semantic information to the top layers. Now is the turn to the attention module. Attention mechanisms are present in different fields, for example in natural language processing the idea behind the attention modules is to focus on a specific part of the input. This illustration represents a convolutional block attention module, in computer vision the input is an image, in human vision if we want to pay attention in a specific object in a scene the human eye brings forward the object that we want to pay attention and blurs the remaining objects or the background. This effect is performed by the application of different filters so in the same way we need to use different filters to extract the information from the input on specific areas where is located the objects that we want to detect. To perform that in this building block we need two sub modules, the channel attention module and the spatial attention module. This is in short SAM, the input feature is an input feature map which is a 3D tensor so first we pass this information to the channel attention module which is in charge to locate the relevant channel between all channels which are present in this input volume. After that we perform the element wise multiplication with the input and this output is fit into the spatial attention module. This spatial attention module is in charge to locate the specific areas in the feature map which are related to the objects that we want to detect. After that we perform again an element wise multiplication to produce the final refined feature map which is a mask. In conclusion we split this 3D volume first analyzing the relevant channels and after that analyzing the specific areas in the spatial attention range related to the objects that we want to detect. We can see in detail these two sub modules. First in the channel attention module we split the input feature map which is a 3D tensor producing two different feature maps, one performing the max pooling operation and the other one performing the average pooling operation. These operations are performed in parallel, after that we pass by different convolutional layers, after that we perform the summing operation for these two feature maps and finally we pass this information to an activation function which is in this case the sigma function with values between zero and one. The final result is an output volume which is a channel attention. Here we have the information what is the relevant channel between all channels present in the input feature volume. So this is for the channel attention module and the spatial attention module is in charge to identify the specific location of the object that we want to detect. So in this case the input of this spatial attention module is a refined feature map because we are going to apply this channel attention mask to the input feature volume which is f. This refined feature map is decomposed in two channels, one performing the max pooling operation and the other one performing the average pooling operation. The output of these layers is feeding to one channel feature map. This feature map has in depth the dimension one. After that we apply the sigma function to produce the final spatial attention feature map. So in conclusion we need to generate different masks applying different convolutional filters and pooling layers for extracting the specific location which is related to the object that we want to detect. This spatial attention module in YOLO version 4 is modified with a new structure. At the top, in this option A, this is a regular sum module but in YOLO version 4 the affords have replaced this max pooling layer and average pooling layer for convolutional layers. The rest of the module is the same. If you want to dive deeper in the attention modules for computer vision you can review this paper for CBAM which is the short name for convolutional block attention module. Now we are going to see another important tool for improving the accuracy which is the Mish activation function. This is Mish activation function which is a family related to ReLU function. There are different functions very similar in the shapes such as ReLU soft+ switch activation function and this Mish activation function. On the left side we have the representation of these functions, on the right side we have the derivative of Mish and switch function. The idea to use this activation function is this negative part because in the positive range the results are the same but using this activation function we can deal in a better way with the negative values and mainly has a property to be non-monotonic function so when you have a monotonic function you always have a regular behavior increasing or decreasing the function but in order to accelerate the learning we need to represent eight different values positive and negative values so we need a non-monotonic nature in the function. The problem with ReLU is on the negative side we have a zero value but this curve helps us very well in the training process because it changes according to the values. This is the reason for this non-monotonic nature in this Mish activation function. In addition if you see here the first derivative is very important when you are performing gradient descent. This red line has higher values than this switch function. We need higher values because we need to accelerate the training process. If this derivative is higher is better for the training. This is the formula is the value multiplied by tan function applied to another function which is soft plus. Soft plus is this the logarithm of this expression and this function is applied in the backbone and also in the detector. So that's all in a quick overview about the theory behind YOLO version 4. There are more techniques for backup freebies and backup fascias but you can review them in the YOLO paper. Now it's time to put in practice this model in the next lesson developing a code in Google Collaboratory. Until then review theory and the paper. See you in the next class, bye bye!
